3.
Selmer complexes, Perrin-Riou logarithm, and Eichler–Shimura morphisms

3.1.
Generalities on Selmer complexes

Suppose that
T
is a representation of
G_{\mathbb{Q}}
over
R
unramified outside a finite set of places
\Sigma
. Suppose that the restriction of
T
to
G_{p}:=G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
fits in a short exact sequence

0\longrightarrow\mathscr{F}^{+}T\longrightarrow T\longrightarrow T/{\mathscr{F}}^{+}T\longrightarrow 0

of
G_{p}
-modules.

3.1.1.
Greenberg local conditions

For
v\in\Sigma
, let
I_{v}\subset G_{v}
be the inertia subgroup and let
\mathrm{Fr}_{v}\in G_{v}/I_{v}
denote the geometric Frobenius element.
We consider the following Greenberg local conditions (in the sense of Nekovář) on
T
on the level of continuous cochains:

U_{v}^{+}(T)=\begin{cases}C^{\bullet}(G_{p},{\mathscr{F}}^{+}T)&\text{ if }v=p,\\
C^{\bullet}(G_{v}/I_{v},T^{I_{v}})&\text{ if }v\in\Sigma\backslash\{p\}.\end{cases}

These are equipped with a morphism of complexes

\iota_{v}^{+}:U_{v}^{+}(T)\longrightarrow C^{\bullet}(G_{v},T),\quad\text{ for all }v\in\Sigma.

We note that when
v\neq p
, the complex
U_{v}^{+}(T)
is quasi-isomorphic to the complex
\Big(T^{I_{v}}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{Fr}_{v}-1}T^{I_{v}}\Big)
concentrated in degrees 0 and 1.

In this paper, we assume that the following
Tamagawa number triviality
hypothesis:

Tam)

H^{1}(I_{v},T)
is a free
R
-module for any prime
v\in\Sigma
.

Under this assumption,
U_{v}^{+}(T)
is perfect for each prime
v\in\Sigma
(see
[
BS25
, §4.3]
), and hence the Selmer complexes that appear later are also perfect.

3.1.2.
Selmer complexes associated to Greenberg local conditions

Let
\Delta
be a Greenberg local condition. We define the Selmer complex associated to
(T,\Sigma,\Delta)
on setting

\widetilde{C}_{\fin}^{\bullet}(G_{\mathbb{Q},\Sigma},T,\Delta):=\cone\Big(C^{\bullet}(G_{\mathbb{Q},\Sigma},T)\oplus U_{\Sigma}^{+}(T)\xrightarrow{\mathrm{res}_{\Sigma}-\iota_{\Sigma}^{+}}C_{\Sigma}^{\bullet}(T)\Big)[-1].

We denote the corresponding object in the derived category by
\mathbf{R}\Gamma_{\fin}(G_{\mathbb{Q},\Sigma},T,\Delta)
and its cohomology by
\mathbf{R}^{\bullet}\Gamma_{\fin}(G_{\mathbb{Q},\Sigma},T,\Delta)
. Recall that we may compute the Euler–Poincaré characteristic of the complex by

\chi(\mathbf{R}\Gamma_{\fin}(G_{\mathbb{Q},\Sigma},T,\Delta))=\mathrm{rank}(T^{c=1})-\mathrm{rank}({\mathscr{F}}^{+}T).

3.2.
The cases of
\mathrm{GL}_{2}
and
\mathrm{GL}_{3}

Let
g
be a normalized cuspidal eigenform of weight
\ell\geq 2
which is new away from
p
, and let
V_{p}(g)
denote its associated Galois representation over a sufficiently large local field
E/\mathbb{Q}_{p}
containing the image of the Hecke field of
g
under the fixed isomorphism
\iota_{p}\colon\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C}_{p}
.
We assume henceforth that
V_{p}(g)
is
p
-ordinary and crystalline.
We let
\mathfrak{a}
denote the unique root of the Hecke polynomial of
g
at
p
which is a
p
-adic unit
11
11

11

More precisely, the
p
-adic valuation of the image of
\mathfrak{a}
under the chain of fixed embedding
\overline{Q}\xrightarrow{\jmath}\mathbb{C}\xrightarrow{\iota_{p}}\mathbb{C}_{p}
is
0
.
. By slight abuse of notation, we denote the unramified character of
G_{p}
mapping the geometric Frobenius to
\mathfrak{a}
also by
\mathfrak{a}
.

3.2.1.

The representation
V_{p}(g)
admits a
G_{p}
-stable filtration

{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}V_{p}(g)\,:V_{p}(g)={\mathscr{F}}^{0}V_{p}(g)\supsetneq{\mathscr{F}}^{1}V_{p}(g)\supsetneq{\mathscr{F}}^{2}V_{p}(g)=\{0\}\,,

where
G_{p}
acts on the graded piece
{\mathscr{G}r}^{0}V_{p}(g)
via
\mathfrak{a}
and via
\chi^{1-\ell}\mathfrak{a}^{-1}
on
{\mathscr{G}r}^{1}V_{p}(g)
.

Moreover,
{\mathscr{F}}^{i}V_{p}(g)
is the exact the annihilator of
{\mathscr{F}}^{2-i}V_{p}(g^{c})
with respect to the Poincaré duality pairing, where
g^{c}(z)=\overline{g(\overline{z})}
is the conjugate form. We remark that we have a natural isomorphism
V_{p}(g)^{*}\simeq V_{p}(g^{c})(\ell-1)
induced from Poincaré duality. We put

{\mathscr{F}}^{i}V_{p}(g)^{*}:={\rm Hom}(V_{p}(g)/{\mathscr{F}}^{2-i}V_{p}(g),E)\simeq{\mathscr{F}}^{i}V_{p}(g^{c})(\ell-1)\,.

3.2.2.

Similarly, the adjoint Galois representation
V_{p}(\ad^{0}(g))=\ker\left(V_{p}(g)\otimes V_{p}(g)^{*}\xrightarrow{\rm ev}E\right)
admits the following filtration of
G_{p}
-modules:

V_{p}(\ad^{0}(g))={\mathscr{F}}^{0}V_{p}(\ad^{0}(g))\supsetneq{\mathscr{F}}^{1}V_{p}(\ad^{0}(g))\supsetneq{\mathscr{F}}^{2}V_{p}(\ad^{0}(g))\supsetneq{\mathscr{F}}^{3}V_{p}(\ad^{0}(g))=\{0\}.

Here

\displaystyle{\mathscr{F}}^{1}V_{p}(\ad^{0}(g))

\displaystyle:=\ker(\ad^{0}(g)\longrightarrow\Hom({\mathscr{F}}^{1}V_{p}(g),{\mathscr{G}}r^{1}V_{p}(g))),

\displaystyle{\mathscr{F}}^{2}V_{p}(\ad^{0}(g))

\displaystyle:={\mathscr{F}}^{1}V_{p}(g)\otimes{\mathscr{F}}^{1}V_{p}(g)^{*}.

Note that the
G_{p}
-action on each of the one-dimensional graded pieces

{\mathscr{G}r}^{0}V_{p}(\ad^{0}(g)):=V_{p}(\ad^{0}(g))/{\mathscr{F}}^{1}V_{p}(\ad^{0}(g)),\quad{\mathscr{G}r}^{1}V_{p}(\ad^{0}(g)):={\mathscr{F}}^{1}V_{p}(\ad^{0}(g))/{\mathscr{F}}^{2}V_{p}(\ad^{0}(g))\,,

{\mathscr{G}r}^{2}V_{p}(\ad^{0}(g)):={\mathscr{F}}^{2}V_{p}(\ad^{0}(g))

is given by
\mathfrak{a}\chi^{\ell-1}
,
\mathds{1}
, and
\mathfrak{a}^{-1}\chi^{1-\ell}
, respectively.

3.2.3.

The analogous definitions are available also when
g
is replaced by our Hida family
\underline{\sigma}
.

3.3.
Siegel modular forms

Let
\Pi
be a cuspidal automorphic representation of
{\rm GSp}_{4}{}_{/\mathbb{Q}}
of weight
(k_{1},k_{2})
with trivial central character. We assume that
k_{1}\geq k_{2}\geq 3
and write
r_{1}=k_{1}-3
and
r_{2}=k_{2}-3
. As in §
2.2
, let
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta)
stand for the Hecke parameters of the Galois representation
V_{p}(\Pi)
, with
v_{p}(\alpha)\leq v_{p}(\beta)\leq v_{p}(\gamma)\leq v_{p}(\delta)
. We also assume that
\Pi
is not a Saito–Kurokawa lift.

We recall that one says that
\Pi_{p}
is Klingen-ordinary if
\frac{\alpha\beta}{p^{k_{2}-2}}
is a
p
-adic unit and that it is Siegel-ordinary if
\alpha
is a
p
-adic unit. We say that it is Borel-ordinary (or simply, ordinary) if it is both Klingen and Siegel ordinary. In that case,
v_{p}(\alpha)=0
,
v_{p}(\beta)=k_{2}-2
,
v_{p}(\gamma)=k_{1}-1
, and
v_{p}(\delta)=k_{1}+k_{2}-3
.

3.3.1.
Duality

We henceforth assume that
\Pi_{p}
is Borel-ordinary. Under the assumptions of §
2.3.8
, the
G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
representation
V_{p}(\Pi)
has a complete flag of
G_{p}
-stable subspaces
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}V_{p}(\Pi)
, where
{\mathscr{F}}^{i}V_{p}(\Pi)
is of codimension
i
(where
0\leq i\leq 4
). Further, following our previous discussion the Hodge–Tate weights of
V_{p}(\Pi)
are
0
,
k_{2}-2
,
k_{1}-1
, and
k_{1}+k_{2}-3
(cf.
[
LSZ22
, Theorem 10.1.3.6]
). Then, as noted in
[
LZ19
, §6.3]
and since the central character
\chi_{\Pi}
of
\Pi
is trivial by assumption, we have a symplectic pairing

V_{p}(\Pi)\otimes V_{p}(\Pi)\longrightarrow E(-k_{1}-k_{2}+3)

induced by Poincaré duality. Under this pairing,
{\mathscr{F}}^{i}V_{p}(\Pi)
is the exact annihilator of
{\mathscr{F}}^{4-i}V_{p}(\Pi)
.

Let us put

V_{p}^{\dagger}(\Pi):=V_{p}(\Pi)\left(\tfrac{k_{1}+k_{2}}{2}-1\right)\,,

so that the pairing above gives rise to the symplectic pairing

V_{p}^{\dagger}(\Pi)\otimes V_{p}^{\dagger}(\Pi)\to E(1)
.

3.4.
Graded pieces

3.4.1.

Let us consider the filtration

V_{p}(\Pi)={\mathscr{F}}^{0}V_{p}(\Pi)\supsetneq{\mathscr{F}}^{1}V_{p}(\Pi)\supsetneq{\mathscr{F}}^{2}V_{p}(\Pi)\supsetneq{\mathscr{F}}^{3}V_{p}(\Pi)\supsetneq{\mathscr{F}}^{4}V_{p}(\Pi)=\{0\},

with graded pieces

\displaystyle\gr^{0}V_{p}(\Pi)={\mathscr{F}}^{0}V_{p}(\Pi)/{\mathscr{F}}^{1}V_{p}(\Pi),

\displaystyle\gr^{1}V_{p}(\Pi)={\mathscr{F}}^{1}V_{p}(\Pi)/{\mathscr{F}}^{2}V_{p}(\Pi),

\displaystyle\gr^{2}V_{p}(\Pi)={\mathscr{F}}^{2}V_{p}(\Pi)/{\mathscr{F}}^{3}V_{p}(\Pi),

\displaystyle\gr^{3}V_{p}(\Pi)={\mathscr{F}}^{3}V_{p}(\Pi)/{\mathscr{F}}^{4}V_{p}(\Pi)\,.

The crystalline Frobenius
\varphi
acts on
{\mathbf{D}}_{\rm cris}(\gr^{0}V_{p}(\Pi)),\ldots,{\mathbf{D}}_{\rm cris}(\gr^{3}V_{p}(\Pi))
by
\alpha
,
\beta
,
\gamma
, and
\delta
, respectively.

3.4.2.
Families of Siegel modular forms

Recall our conventions for (two-variable) families of ordinary Siegel modular forms fixed in
2.3
. The analogous definitions are available also when
\Pi
is replaced by a Hida family
{\underline{\Pi}}
, satisfying both the Klingen and Siegel ordinary conditions, as discussed in
[
LZ20
, §23]
and
[
LZ21a
, §10]
. In particular, we may still consider a filtration

T_{\underline{\Pi}}={\mathscr{F}}^{0}T_{\underline{\Pi}}\supsetneq{\mathscr{F}}^{1}T_{\underline{\Pi}}\supsetneq{\mathscr{F}}^{2}T_{\underline{\Pi}}\supsetneq{\mathscr{F}}^{3}T_{\underline{\Pi}}\supsetneq{\mathscr{F}}^{4}T_{\underline{\Pi}}=\{0\},

as well as the corresponding graded pieces

\displaystyle\gr^{0}T_{\underline{\Pi}}={\mathscr{F}}^{0}T_{\underline{\Pi}}/{\mathscr{F}}^{1}T_{\underline{\Pi}},

\displaystyle\gr^{1}T_{\underline{\Pi}}={\mathscr{F}}^{1}T_{\underline{\Pi}}/{\mathscr{F}}^{2}T_{\underline{\Pi}},

\displaystyle\gr^{2}T_{\underline{\Pi}}={\mathscr{F}}^{2}T_{\underline{\Pi}}/{\mathscr{F}}^{3}T_{\underline{\Pi}},

\displaystyle\gr^{3}T_{\underline{\Pi}}={\mathscr{F}}^{3}T_{\underline{\Pi}}/{\mathscr{F}}^{4}T_{\underline{\Pi}}\,.

As before, let
({\mathbf{k}}_{1},\,{\mathbf{k}}_{2})
be the universal characters associated to the 2-dimensional weight space
\Omega
. We set

T^{\dagger}_{\underline{\Pi}}:=T_{\underline{\Pi}}\left(\tfrac{{\mathbf{k}}_{1}+{\mathbf{k}}_{2}}{2}-1\right).

Then there is a skew-symmetric pairing

T_{\underline{\Pi}}\otimes T_{\underline{\Pi}}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{R}}_{{\underline{\Pi}}}({\mathbf{k}}_{1}+{\mathbf{k}}_{2}-3),

with respect to which
{\mathscr{F}}^{i}T_{\underline{\Pi}}
is the exact annihilator of
{\mathscr{F}}^{4-i}T_{\underline{\Pi}}
; equivalently, there is a skew-symmetric pairing

T^{\dagger}_{\underline{\Pi}}\otimes T^{\dagger}_{\underline{\Pi}}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{R}}_{{\underline{\Pi}}}(1).

3.5.
Triple products

With the assumptions of
3.2
and
3.3
, let
g
be a modular forms of weight
\ell
and
\Pi
a cuspidal representation of weight
(k_{1},k_{2})
with trivial central characters and
k_{1}\geq k_{2}\geq 3
. Let us put
c:=\frac{k_{1}+k_{2}}{2}+\ell-2
.
Let

V:=V_{p}(\Pi)\otimes V_{p}(g)\otimes V_{p}(g^{c})(c)\simeq V_{p}^{\dagger}(\Pi)\otimes V_{p}(g)\otimes V_{p}(g)^{*}.

We also define a Galois stable
{\mathcal{O}}_{E}
-lattice
T\subset V
, which comes associated with the choice of lattices
T_{?}\subset V_{p}(?)
(which we henceforth fix). We put

M:=T_{\Pi}^{\dagger}\otimes\ad^{0}T_{g},\,\quad M[1/p]:=V_{p}^{\dagger}(\Pi)\otimes\ad^{0}V_{p}(g).

3.5.1.

We shall describe the corresponding Greenberg local conditions associated with the regions of weights described in
[
LZ21b
, §4]
. These will be given in terms of
G_{p}
-stable subspaces of
V
of dimension
8
, as described in the definition below. To highlight their relevance to the corresponding region, we shall notate them by
F_{?}^{+}X
with
?=a,b,c,d,e
.

Definition 3.1
.

We put

\displaystyle F_{a}^{+}V

\displaystyle:=V_{p}^{\dagger}(\Pi)\otimes{\mathscr{F}}^{1}V_{p}(g)\otimes V_{p}(g)^{*}\,,

\displaystyle F_{b}^{+}V

\displaystyle:={\mathscr{F}}^{1}V_{p}^{\dagger}(\Pi)\otimes{\mathscr{F}}^{1}V_{p}(g)\otimes V_{p}(g)^{*}+{\mathscr{F}}^{3}V_{p}^{\dagger}(\Pi)\otimes V_{p}(g)\otimes{\mathscr{F}}^{1}V_{p}(g)^{*}

\displaystyle\hskip 170.71652pt+V_{p}^{\dagger}(\Pi)\otimes{\mathscr{F}}^{1}V_{p}(g)\otimes{\mathscr{F}}^{1}V_{p}(g)^{*}\,,

\displaystyle F_{c}^{+}V

\displaystyle:=V_{p}^{\dagger}(\Pi)\otimes{\mathscr{F}}^{1}V_{p}(g)\otimes{\mathscr{F}}^{1}V_{p}(g)^{*}+{\mathscr{F}}^{2}V_{p}^{\dagger}(\Pi)\otimes{\mathscr{F}}^{1}V_{p}(g)\otimes V_{p}(g)^{*}

\displaystyle\hskip 170.71652pt+{\mathscr{F}}^{2}V_{p}^{\dagger}(\Pi)\otimes V_{p}(g)\otimes{\mathscr{F}}^{1}V_{p}(g)^{*}\,,

\displaystyle F_{d}^{+}V

\displaystyle:={\mathscr{F}}^{1}V_{p}^{\dagger}(\Pi)\otimes{\mathscr{F}}^{1}V_{p}(g)\otimes V_{p}(g)^{*}+{\mathscr{F}}^{3}V_{p}^{\dagger}(\Pi)\otimes V_{p}(g)\otimes V_{p}(g)^{*}\,,

\displaystyle F_{e}^{+}V

\displaystyle:={\mathscr{F}}^{3}V_{p}^{\dagger}(\Pi)\otimes V_{p}(g)\otimes V_{p}(g)^{*}+{\mathscr{F}}^{1}V_{p}^{\dagger}(\Pi)\otimes{\mathscr{F}}^{1}V_{p}(g)\otimes{\mathscr{F}}^{1}V_{p}(g)^{*}

\displaystyle\qquad+{\mathscr{F}}^{2}V_{p}^{\dagger}(\Pi)\otimes{\mathscr{F}}^{1}V_{p}(g)\otimes V_{p}(g)^{*}+{\mathscr{F}}^{2}V_{p}^{\dagger}(\Pi)\otimes V_{p}(g)\otimes{\mathscr{F}}^{1}V_{p}(g)^{*}\,,

We analogously define
F_{?}^{+}T
, and put

\displaystyle F^{+}_{?}M

\displaystyle:=\im(F_{?}^{+}T\hookrightarrow T\xrightarrow{\rm tr^{*}}M)\,,\qquad?=a,b,c,d,e\,,

where
\rm tr^{*}
is the map induced from the dual of the map
{\rm ad}^{0}T_{g}=\ker({\rm tr})\hookrightarrow{\rm End}(T_{g})\simeq T_{g}\otimes T_{g}^{*}
.

Lemma 3.2
.

We have the following isomorphisms of
G_{p}
-representations.

\displaystyle F_{a}^{+}T/(F_{a}^{+}T\cap F_{b}^{+}T)

\displaystyle\simeq{\mathscr{G}r}^{0}T^{\dagger}_{\Pi}\otimes{\mathscr{F}}^{1}T_{g}\otimes{\mathscr{G}r}^{0}T_{g}^{*}\simeq{\mathscr{G}r}^{0}T^{\dagger}_{\Pi}\,,

\displaystyle F_{b}^{+}T/(F_{c}^{+}T\cap F_{b}^{+}T)

\displaystyle\simeq{\mathscr{G}r}^{1}T^{\dagger}_{\Pi}\otimes{\mathscr{F}}^{1}T_{g}\otimes{\mathscr{G}r}^{0}T_{g}^{*}\simeq{\mathscr{G}r}^{1}T^{\dagger}_{\Pi}

Proof.

Observing that

F_{a}^{+}T\cap F_{b}^{+}T={\mathscr{F}}^{1}T^{\dagger}_{\Pi}\otimes{\mathscr{F}}^{1}T_{g}\otimes T_{g}^{*}+T^{\dagger}_{\Pi}\otimes{\mathscr{F}}^{1}T_{g}\otimes{\mathscr{F}}^{1}T_{g}^{*}

and

F_{c}^{+}T\cap F_{b}^{+}T=T^{\dagger}_{\Pi}\otimes{\mathscr{F}}^{1}T_{g}\otimes{\mathscr{F}}^{1}T_{g}^{*}+{\mathscr{F}}^{2}T^{\dagger}_{\Pi}\otimes{\mathscr{F}}^{1}T_{g}\otimes T_{g}^{*}+{\mathscr{F}}^{3}T^{\dagger}_{\Pi}\otimes T_{g}\otimes{\mathscr{F}}^{1}T_{g}^{*}\,,

the asserted isomorphisms follow from a direct calculation.
∎

3.5.2.

The analogous constructions are available when
\Pi
is replaced by a Hida family
{\underline{\Pi}}
of Siegel modular forms, and
g
replaced by a primitive Hida family
\underline{\sigma}
of elliptic modular forms. We note that the conjugate family
\underline{\sigma}^{c}
is defined as
\underline{\sigma}\otimes{\chi}_{\underline{\sigma}}^{-1}
, where
{\chi}_{\underline{\sigma}}
is the tame nebentype of the family
\underline{\sigma}
.

3.5.3.
Greenberg local conditions for families

Definition 3.3
.

For
?\in\{a,b,c,d,e\}
, let

F_{?}^{+}T_{3}^{{\dagger}}:=F_{?}^{+}T_{4}^{{\dagger}}\otimes_{\iota_{3,4}^{*}}\mathcal{R}_{3}.

We also define the Greenberg-local condition
{\mathrm{tr}}^{*}\Delta_{?}=\Delta(F_{?}^{+}M_{3}^{{\dagger}})
by

F_{?}^{+}M_{3}^{{\dagger}}:=\im(F_{?}^{+}T_{3}^{{\dagger}}\hookrightarrow T_{3}^{{\dagger}}\rightarrow M_{3}^{{\dagger}}).

Lemma 3.4
.

We have

\mathrm{rank}_{\mathcal{R}_{\underline{\sigma}}}F_{a}^{+}M_{3}^{{\dagger}}=8,\quad\mathrm{rank}_{\mathcal{R}_{\underline{\sigma}}}F_{b}^{+}M_{3}^{{\dagger}}=7,\quad\mathrm{rank}_{\mathcal{R}_{\underline{\sigma}}}F_{c}^{+}M_{3}^{{\dagger}}=6,

\mathrm{rank}_{\mathcal{R}_{\underline{\sigma}}}F_{d}^{+}M_{3}^{{\dagger}}=7,\quad\mathrm{rank}_{\mathcal{R}_{\underline{\sigma}}}F_{e}^{+}M_{3}^{{\dagger}}=6.

We have the following natural short exact sequences

0\longrightarrow F_{b}^{+}M_{3}^{{\dagger}}\longrightarrow F_{a}^{+}M_{3}^{{\dagger}}\longrightarrow\gr^{0}T^{\dagger}_{\underline{\Pi}}\otimes\gr^{1}\ad^{0}(T_{\underline{\sigma}})\longrightarrow 0,

0\longrightarrow F_{c}^{+}M_{3}^{{\dagger}}\longrightarrow F_{b}^{+}M_{3}^{{\dagger}}\longrightarrow\gr^{1}T^{\dagger}_{\underline{\Pi}}\otimes\gr^{1}\ad^{0}(T_{\underline{\sigma}})\longrightarrow 0,

and

0\longrightarrow F_{e}^{+}M_{3}^{{\dagger}}\longrightarrow F_{d}^{+}M_{3}^{{\dagger}}\longrightarrow\gr^{1}T^{\dagger}_{\underline{\Pi}}\otimes\gr^{1}\ad^{0}(T_{\underline{\sigma}})\longrightarrow 0.

Proof.

This follows from the definitions. ∎

3.6.
Selmer complexes (bis)

For
?=a,b,c,d,e
, let us denote by
\Delta_{a}
the Greenberg local conditions (in the sense of §
3.1.1
) on
T_{3}^{\dagger}
given by
\iota_{p}^{+}:F_{?}^{+}T_{3}^{\dagger}\hookrightarrow T_{3}^{\dagger}
. We denote by
{\rm tr}^{*}\Delta_{?}
the Greenberg local condition on
M_{3}^{\dagger}
that is given by
\iota_{p}^{+}:F_{?}^{+}M_{3}^{\dagger}\hookrightarrow M_{3}^{\dagger}
.

Definition 3.5
.

Let us denote by
\mathrm{res}_{a/b}
the composition

\displaystyle\mathbf{R}^{1}\Gamma_{\fin}(G_{\mathbb{Q},\Sigma},M_{3}^{{\dagger}},{\mathrm{tr}}^{*}\Delta_{a})\xrightarrow{\mathrm{res}_{p}}H_{\fin}^{1}(G_{p},F_{a}^{+}M_{3}^{{\dagger}})\longrightarrow H^{1}(G_{p},F_{a}^{+}M_{3}^{{\dagger}}/F_{b}^{+}M_{3}^{{\dagger}})\simeq

\displaystyle\,H^{1}(G_{p},{\mathscr{G}r}^{0}T^{\dagger}_{\underline{\Pi}}\,\widehat{\otimes}\gr^{1}\ad^{0}(T_{\underline{\sigma}}))

\displaystyle\quad\simeq H^{1}(G_{p},{\mathscr{G}r}^{0}T^{\dagger}_{\underline{\Pi}})\otimes_{{\mathcal{R}}_{{\underline{\Pi}}}}{\mathcal{R}}_{3}\,,

where the first map is given by

\displaystyle\mathbf{R}^{1}\Gamma_{\fin}(G_{\mathbb{Q},\Sigma},M_{3}^{{\dagger}},{\mathrm{tr}}^{*}\Delta_{a})\ni[c,(c_{v}),(\lambda_{v})]\longmapsto\mathrm{res}_{p}([c])=\iota_{p}^{+}([c_{p}])\in H^{1}(G_{p},F_{a}^{+}M_{3}^{\dagger})\,,

the third arises from Lemma
3.4
(b), and the final isomorphism follows from the fact that
\gr^{1}\ad^{0}(T_{\underline{\sigma}})\simeq R_{\underline{\sigma}}
as
G_{p}
-modules, as explained in §
3.2.2
. Similarly, we define the maps

\mathrm{res}_{b/c}\,:\,\mathbf{R}^{1}\Gamma_{\fin}(G_{\mathbb{Q},\Sigma},M_{3}^{{\dagger}},{\mathrm{tr}}^{*}\Delta_{b})\longrightarrow H^{1}(G_{p},F_{b}^{+}M_{3}^{{\dagger}}/F_{c}^{+}M_{3}^{{\dagger}})\simeq H^{1}(G_{p},{\mathscr{G}r}^{1}T^{\dagger}_{\underline{\Pi}})\widehat{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{R}}_{{\underline{\Pi}}}}{\mathcal{R}}_{3}\,,

\mathrm{res}_{d/e}\,:\,\mathbf{R}^{1}\Gamma_{\fin}(G_{\mathbb{Q},\Sigma},M_{3}^{{\dagger}},{\mathrm{tr}}^{*}\Delta_{d})\longrightarrow H^{1}(G_{p},F_{d}^{+}M_{3}^{{\dagger}}/F_{e}^{+}M_{3}^{{\dagger}})\simeq H^{1}(G_{p},{\mathscr{G}r}^{1}T^{\dagger}_{\underline{\Pi}})\widehat{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{R}}_{{\underline{\Pi}}}}{\mathcal{R}}_{3}\,.

3.7.
Eichler–Shimura isomorphisms for families of Siegel modular forms

In this subsection, we discuss how to naturally trivialise the
p
-local cohomology of the graded pieces of the Galois representations associated with families of Siegel modular forms, relying on the Eichler–Shimura isomorphisms proved in
[
LZ21a
, §11]
. As usual, let
L
be a
p
-adic field. We borrow the following definition from Definition 10.4.1. of op. cit.

Definition 3.6
.

For a fixed family
{\underline{\Pi}}
, let
S^{i}({\underline{\Pi}})
be the
{\underline{\Pi}}
-eigenspace in the degree-
i
coherent cohomology of the corresponding Siegel threefold, and with values in the
p
-adic field
L
.

\blacksquare

Remark 3.7
.

These spaces are 1-dimensional for each
i\in\{0,1,2,3\}
for “generic” families of Siegel forms, whereas for those of Yoshida type, they are 1-dimensional for
i=1,2
, and 0-dimensional when
i=0,3
.

\blacksquare

3.7.1.

Let us fix a crystalline specialisation
\Pi
of
{\underline{\Pi}}
of weight
(r_{1}+3,r_{2}+3)
, with
r_{1}\geq r_{2}
. Then
{\mathbf{D}}_{\rm cris}(V_{p}(\Pi))
is endowed with a decreasing Hodge filtration

\{0\}=\mathrm{Fil}_{\text{Hdg}}^{r_{1}+r_{2}+4}V_{p}(\Pi)\subset\cdots\subset\mathrm{Fil}_{\text{Hdg}}^{i}V_{p}(\Pi)\subset\mathrm{Fil}_{\text{Hdg}}^{i-1}V_{p}(\Pi)\subset\cdots\subset\mathrm{Fil}_{\text{Hdg}}^{0}V_{p}(\Pi):={\mathbf{D}}_{\rm cris}(V_{p}(\Pi))\,,

with
\Gr_{\text{Hdg}}^{i}V_{p}(\Pi):=\mathrm{Fil}_{\text{Hdg}}^{i}V_{p}(\Pi)/\mathrm{Fil}_{\text{Hdg}}^{i+1}V_{p}(\Pi)
for
i=0,r_{2}+1,r_{1}+2,r_{1}+r_{2}+3
(which are the Hodge–Tate weights of this Galois representation) the non-trivial graded pieces.

3.7.2.

Until §
6
(where we treat endoscopic cases), we shall work under the following assumption. As we shall explain, it is especially crucial for the statement of Eichler–Shimura isomorphism (Proposition
3.9
below).

Assumption 3.8
.

The family
12
12

12

or rather, its
p
-refinement
({\underline{\Pi}},\mathscr{F}=\{\mathscr{F}^{i}\})
.

{\underline{\Pi}}
does not admit any critical specialisations, in the sense of Bellaïche–Chenevier. This means, for any crystalline specialisation
\Pi
of
{\underline{\Pi}}
of weight
(r_{1}+3,r_{2}+3)
with
r_{1}\geq r_{2}
, the following holds true:

(3.1)

\mathrm{Fil}_{\text{Hdg}}^{r_{i}+3-i}V_{p}(\Pi)\cap\mathscr{F}^{i+1}V_{p}(\Pi)=\{0\}=\mathrm{Fil}_{\text{Hdg}}^{r_{1}+r_{2}+3}V_{p}(\Pi)\cap\mathscr{F}^{1}V_{p}(\Pi)\,,\qquad i=1,2\,.

\blacksquare

3.7.3.

We briefly review Eichler–Shimura morphisms at a fixed weight. To that end, let us fix a crystalline specialisation
\Pi
of
{\underline{\Pi}}
as in §
3.7.2
, and let us assume that its
p
-refinement given by
\{\mathscr{F}^{i}V_{p}(\Pi)\}
verifies (
3.1
) (i.e., this refined pair is non-critical). As usual, we let
\varphi
denote the crystalline Frobenius acting on
V_{p}(\Pi)
, and we let
\alpha,\ldots,\delta
be the Hecke parameters of
\Pi
at
p
, given as in Section
3.3
(which coincide with the eigenvalues of
\varphi
). We then have the isomorphisms

(3.2)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} S^{0}(\Pi,L)&\simeq{\rm Gr}^{r_{1}+r_{2}+3}_{\rm Hdg}\,V_{p}(\Pi)\,,\qquad S^{3}(\Pi,L)\simeq{\rm Gr}^{0}_{\rm Hdg}\,V_{p}(\Pi)\,,\\
S^{i}(\Pi,L)&\simeq{\rm Gr}^{r_{i}+3-i}_{\rm Hdg}\,V_{p}(\Pi)\,,\qquad i=1,2\,,\end{aligned}

induced from the comparison isomorphisms of Faltings and Tsuji (cf.
[
LZ21a
]
, §11.4). Thanks to our running non-criticality assumption (
3.1
), we have the following isomorphisms:

(3.3)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} {\rm Gr}^{r_{1}+r_{2}+3}_{\rm Hdg}V_{p}(\Pi)={\rm Fil}^{r_{1}+r_{2}+3}_{\rm Hdg}\,V_{p}(\Pi)\xrightarrow{\,\sim\,}{\mathbf{D}}_{\rm cris}(V_{p}(\Pi))/{\mathbf{D}}_{\rm cris}(\mathscr{F}^{1}V_{p}(\Pi))={\mathbf{D}}_{\rm cris}(\gr^{0}V_{p}(\Pi))\,,\\
{\mathbf{D}}_{\rm cris}(\gr^{3}V_{p}(\Pi))={\mathbf{D}}_{\rm cris}(\mathscr{F}^{3}V_{p}(\Pi))\xrightarrow{\,\sim\,}{\mathbf{D}}_{\rm cris}(V_{p}(\Pi))/{\rm Fil}^{r_{1}+2}_{\rm Hdg}V_{p}(\Pi)={\rm Gr}^{0}_{\rm Hdg}V_{p}(\Pi)\,,\\
\\
{\rm Fil}^{r_{i}+3-i}_{\rm Hdg}V_{p}(\Pi)\cap{\mathbf{D}}_{\rm cris}(\mathscr{F}^{i}V_{p}(\Pi))\xrightarrow{\sim}{\rm Fil}^{r_{i}+3-i}_{\rm Hdg}V_{p}(\Pi)/{\rm Fil}^{r_{1}+r_{2}-r_{i}+i}_{\rm Hdg}V_{p}(\Pi)={\rm Gr}^{r_{i}+3-i}_{\rm Hdg}V_{p}(\Pi)\\
\xrightarrow{\sim}{\mathbf{D}}_{\rm cris}(\mathscr{F}^{i}V_{p}(\Pi))/{\mathbf{D}}_{\rm cris}(\mathscr{F}^{i+1}V_{p}(\Pi))={\mathbf{D}}_{\rm cris}(\gr^{i}V_{p}(\Pi))\,,\,\quad i=1,2\,.\end{aligned}

Combining (
3.2
) with (
3.3
), we arrive at the following (punctual) Eichler–Shimura isomorphisms:

(3.4)

\displaystyle S^{i}(\Pi,L)

\displaystyle\simeq{\mathbf{D}}_{\rm cris}(\gr^{i}V_{p}(\Pi)),\qquad i=0,1,2,3\,.

3.7.4.

We record below the following result due to Loeffler and Zerbes (extending the work of Diao–Rosso–Wu in
[
DRW21
]
; see also
[
DRW25a
]
), on the interpolation of the Eichler–Shimura morphisms (
3.4
) in families of Siegel modular forms.

Let
R
be a domain and let
X
be a free
R
-module of rank one, which is equipped with a continuous
G_{p}
-action, such that the action of
G_{p}
on
X\otimes\eta^{-1}
is unramified for some character
\eta
of
\Gamma_{\cyc}
. In this scenario, we put
X^{\circ}:=X\otimes\eta^{-1}
and define (rather abusively)
{\mathbf{D}}_{\cris}(X):={\mathbf{D}}_{\cris}(X^{\circ})
. We remark that
X={\mathscr{G}r}^{i}T_{\underline{\Pi}}
have this property.

Proposition 3.9

(Loeffler–Zerbes)
.

Under Assumption
3.8
, we have isomorphisms

\ES^{i}\colon S^{i}({\underline{\Pi}})\cong{\mathbf{D}}_{\cris}\left({\mathscr{G}r}^{i}T_{\underline{\Pi}}\right)\,,\qquad\forall\,i\in\{0,1,2,3\}\,.

Proof.

This follows from the discussion of
[
LZ21a
, Remark 11.4.1]
(see also
[
LZ21a
, Theorem 11.6.3]
).
∎

Remark 3.10
.

The condition
3.1
is not explicitly assumed in
[
LZ21a
]
, but it is rather implied by the underlying assumptions in op. cit. More precisely, the authors explain that the Eichler–Shimura isomorphisms (
3.4
) can be interpolated over the locus of
good points
, cf.
[
LZ21a
, Definition 10.4.2]
. Then a good Borel-ordinary point of regular weight as in op. cit. is necessarily of numerically-non-critical (in the sense of
[
BC09
]
, Remark 2.4.6), and therefore non-critical (i.e. it verifies Assumption
3.8
). Our emphasis on this assumption is due to our interest in formulating Eichler–Shimura isomorphisms in the form of (
3.4
).
\blacksquare

3.7.5.

Note that we have a pairing

(3.5)

\displaystyle\langle\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,\rangle\,\colon

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathbf{D}}_{\cris}({\mathscr{G}r}^{i}T_{\underline{\Pi}}({\mathbf{r}}_{1}+{\mathbf{r}}_{2}+3))\times{\mathbf{D}}_{\cris}({\mathscr{G}r}^{3-i}T_{\underline{\Pi}})\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}(U)\,,\qquad i=0,1,2,3\,,

that comes about interpolating Poincaré duality pairings (cf.
[
LZ21b
]
, §6.3).

Definition 3.11
.

For any
\eta\in S^{i}({\underline{\Pi}})
, we denote by

\ES_{{\underline{\Pi}}}^{i}(\eta)\,\colon\,{\mathbf{D}}_{\cris}({\mathscr{G}r}^{3-i}T_{\underline{\Pi}})\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}(U)

the homomorphism induced from the pairing (
3.5
).
\blacksquare

We henceforth fix a choice of a
{\mathcal{R}}_{\underline{\Pi}}
-basis
\{\eta_{i}\}
of
S^{i}({\underline{\Pi}},L)
.

4.
Trivialisations and modules of leading terms

We recall in this section the notion of the module of leading terms of algebraic
p
-adic
L
-functions introduced in
[
BS25
, §5]
, as well its key properties. We will review the main general constructions in op. cit. §
4.2
at a great level of generality. We will then apply these in §
5
to the factorisation problem at hand.

We also refer the reader to
[
BS25
, Remark 5.14]
for the connection of the module of leading terms to
p
-adic
L
-functions.

4.1.
The setup

For any commutative ring
S
and any finitely generated
S
-module
M
, we put

M^{*}:=\Hom_{S}(M,S)\,\,\,\textrm{ and }\,\,\,{\bigcap}^{t}_{S}M:=\left({\bigwedge}^{t}_{S}M^{*}\right)^{*}\,,

and call
{\bigcap}^{t}_{S}M
the
t
-th exterior bi-dual of
M
.

Remark 4.1
.

When
G
is a finte abelian group and
S=\mathbb{Z}[G]
, the
t
-th exterior bi-dual
{\bigcap}^{t}_{S}M
of
M
is canonically isomorphic to the Rubin’s lattice
\wedge^{t}_{0}M
of
M
defined in
[
Rub96
]
.
The exterior bi-dual is a natural generalisation of the Rubin’s lattice.

\blacksquare

Let
R
be a complete Noetherian local ring with finite residue field of characteristic
p\geq 3
and
T
be a free
R
-module of finite rank with a continuous
G_{\mathbb{Q},\Sigma}
-action.

Throughout §
4.2
, we assume that
we have an
R[G_{p}]
-submodule
{\mathscr{F}}^{+}T
of
T
such that the quotient
T/{\mathscr{F}}^{+}T
is free as an
R
-module.
Then we have the Greenberg local conditions
\Delta:=\Delta_{\mathscr{F}^{+}}
; cf. §
3.1.1
.
We also assume throughout §
4.2
that the condition (
Tam)
) holds. For simplicity, let us put

r=r(T,\Delta):=-\chi(\mathbf{R}\Gamma_{\rm f}(G_{\mathbb{Q},\Sigma},T,\Delta))=\mathrm{rank}_{R}(T^{c=-1})-\mathrm{rank}_{R}(T/{\mathscr{F}}^{+}T)\in\mathbb{Z}.

4.2.
Modules of leading terms

Definition 4.2
.

We let

\delta(T,\Delta)\subset{\bigcap}^{r}_{R}\mathbf{R}^{1}\Gamma_{\fin}(G_{\mathbb{Q},\Sigma},T,\Delta)

denote the module of leading terms introduced in
[
BS25
, §5.2]
.
\blacksquare

The first key properties of the module
\delta(T,\Delta)
are recorded below.

Theorem 4.3
.

The
R
-module
\mathbf{R}^{2}\Gamma_{\fin}(G_{\mathbb{Q},\Sigma},T,\Delta)
is torsion if and only if
\delta(T,\Delta)
is generated by an
R
-regular element of
{\bigcap}^{r}_{R}\mathbf{R}^{1}\Gamma_{\fin}(G_{\mathbb{Q},\Sigma},T,\Delta)
.

Suppose in addition that
R
is normal.
If
\delta(T,\Delta)\neq 0
, then the
R
-module
\mathbf{R}^{2}\Gamma_{\fin}(G_{\mathbb{Q},\Sigma},T,\Delta)
is torsion and

\mathrm{char}_{R}\left(\left.{\bigcap}^{r}_{R}\mathbf{R}^{1}\Gamma_{\fin}(G_{\mathbb{Q},\Sigma},T,\Delta)\middle/\delta(T,\Delta)\right.\right)=\mathrm{char}_{R}\left(\mathbf{R}^{2}\Gamma_{\fin}(G_{\mathbb{Q},\Sigma},T,\Delta)\right).

For any flat ring homomorphism
R\rightarrow S
, we have
\delta(T,\Delta)\otimes_{R}S=\delta(T\otimes_{R}S,\Delta)
.

Proof.

This is Proposition 5.5 and Theorem 5.6 of
[
BS25
]
.
∎

4.2.1.

We assume that we have an
R[G_{p}]
-submodule
{\mathscr{F}}T
of
{\mathscr{F}}^{+}T
such that

•

the quotient
{\mathscr{F}}^{+}T/{\mathscr{F}}T
is free as an
R
-module and

•

H^{0}(G_{p},{\mathscr{F}}^{+}\overline{T}/{\mathscr{F}}\overline{T})=H^{2}(G_{p},{\mathscr{F}}^{+}\overline{T}/{\mathscr{F}}\overline{T})=0
, where
\overline{T}
denotes the residual representation of
T
.

Now we have two Greenberg local conditions
\Delta_{\mathscr{F}^{+}}
and
\Delta_{\mathscr{F}}
.
For simplicity, let us put

r:=r(T,\Delta_{{\mathscr{F}}^{+}})\,\,\,\textrm{ and }\,\,\,s:=r(T,\Delta_{\mathscr{F}}).

We also suppose that

•

s\geq 0
.

Since
H^{0}(G_{p},\mathscr{F}^{+}\overline{T}/\mathscr{F}\overline{T})=H^{2}(G_{p},\mathscr{F}^{+}\overline{T}/\mathscr{F}\overline{T})=0
by assumption, the
R
-module
H^{1}(G_{p},{\mathscr{F}}^{+}T/{\mathscr{F}}T)
is free of rank
r-s
.
Fix an
R
-isomorphism

\varphi:=\bigoplus_{1\leq i\leq r-s}\varphi_{i}\colon H^{1}(G_{p},{\mathscr{F}}^{+}T/{\mathscr{F}}T)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}R^{r-s},

where
\varphi_{i}\in\Hom_{R}(H^{1}(G_{p},{\mathscr{F}}^{+}T/{\mathscr{F}}T),R)
.
Then the homomorphisms
\varphi_{1},\ldots,\varphi_{r-s}
induce an
R
-homomorphism

\displaystyle\Phi\colon{\bigcap}_{R}^{r}\mathbf{R}^{1}\Gamma_{\fin}(G_{\mathbb{Q},\Sigma},T,\Delta_{{\mathscr{F}}^{+}})\longrightarrow{\bigcap}_{R}^{s}\mathbf{R}^{1}\Gamma_{\fin}(G_{\mathbb{Q},\Sigma},T,\Delta_{\mathscr{F}})

given by

\Phi(x):=x(-\wedge(\varphi_{1}\circ\mathrm{res}_{p})\wedge\cdots\wedge(\varphi_{r-s}\circ\mathrm{res}_{p})).

Lemma 4.4
.

When
R
is a discrete valuation ring, we have

\Phi(\delta(T,\Delta_{\mathscr{F}^{+}}))=\delta(T,\Delta_{\mathscr{F}}).

Proof.

When

\mathrm{rank}_{R}\im(\varphi\circ\mathrm{res}_{p}\colon\mathbf{R}^{1}\Gamma_{\fin}(G_{\mathbb{Q},\Sigma},T,\Delta_{{\mathscr{F}}^{+}})\longrightarrow R^{r-s})<r-s,

we have
(\varphi_{1}\circ\mathrm{res}_{p})\wedge\cdots\wedge(\varphi_{r-s}\circ\mathrm{res}_{p})=0
, and hence

\Phi(\delta(T,\Delta_{\mathscr{F}^{+}}))=0.

Since
\mathrm{rank}_{R}\mathbf{R}^{2}\Gamma_{\fin}(G_{\mathbb{Q},\Sigma},T,\Delta_{\mathscr{F}})\geq\mathrm{rank}_{R}\coker(\varphi\circ\mathrm{res}_{p})\geq 1
, the
R
-module
\mathbf{R}^{2}\Gamma_{\fin}(G_{\mathbb{Q},\Sigma},T,\Delta_{\mathscr{F}})
is not torsion. Hence Theorem
4.3
(i) shows
\delta(T,\Delta_{\mathscr{F}})=0
.
Therefore, we obtain
\Phi(\delta(T,\Delta_{\mathscr{F}^{+}}))=0=\delta(T,\Delta_{\mathscr{F}})
.

Suppose that
\mathrm{rank}_{R}\im(\varphi\circ\mathrm{res}_{p})=r-s
.
We then have

\mathrm{rank}_{R}\mathbf{R}^{2}\Gamma_{\fin}(G_{\mathbb{Q},\Sigma},T,\Delta_{\mathscr{F}})=\mathrm{rank}_{R}\mathbf{R}^{2}\Gamma_{\fin}(G_{\mathbb{Q},\Sigma},T,\Delta_{\mathscr{F}^{+}}).

If
\mathrm{rank}_{R}\mathbf{R}^{2}\Gamma_{\fin}(G_{\mathbb{Q},\Sigma},T,\Delta_{\mathscr{F}^{+}})>0
, then Theorem
4.3
(i) shows
\Phi(\delta(T,\Delta_{\mathscr{F}^{+}}))=0=\delta(T,\Delta_{\mathscr{F}})
.
Hence we may assume that both
\mathbf{R}^{2}\Gamma_{\fin}(G_{\mathbb{Q},\Sigma},T,\Delta_{\mathscr{F}})
and
\mathbf{R}^{2}\Gamma_{\fin}(G_{\mathbb{Q},\Sigma},T,\Delta_{\mathscr{F}^{+}})
are torsion.
Put

\displaystyle\ell_{\mathscr{F}}

\displaystyle:=\mathrm{length}_{R}\mathbf{R}^{2}\Gamma_{\fin}(G_{\mathbb{Q},\Sigma},T,\Delta_{\mathscr{F}})<+\infty,

\displaystyle\ell_{{\mathscr{F}}^{+}}

\displaystyle:=\mathrm{length}_{R}\mathbf{R}^{2}\Gamma_{\fin}(G_{\mathbb{Q},\Sigma},T,\Delta_{\mathscr{F}^{+}})<+\infty.

Since
R
is a discrete valuation ring, we have isomorphisms

\mathbf{R}^{1}\Gamma_{\fin}(G_{\mathbb{Q},\Sigma},T,\Delta_{\mathscr{F}})\cong R^{s}\,\,\,\textrm{ and }\,\,\,\mathbf{R}^{1}\Gamma_{\fin}(G_{\mathbb{Q},\Sigma},T,\Delta_{\mathscr{F}^{+}})\cong R^{r}.

Moreover, by replacing
\varphi
with other isomorphism if necessary, one can take a basis
\{e_{1},\ldots,e_{r}\}
of
\mathbf{R}^{1}\Gamma_{\fin}(G_{\mathbb{Q},\Sigma},T,\Delta_{\mathscr{F}^{+}})
such that

\displaystyle\varphi_{i}\circ\mathrm{res}_{p}(e_{j})=\pi^{a_{i}}\delta_{i+s,j}.

Here
\delta_{i+s,j}
denotes the Kronecker’s delta,
\pi
is a unifomizer of
R
, and
a_{i}
is a non-negative integer. Note that
a_{1}+\cdots+a_{r-s}=\ell_{F}-\ell_{F^{+}}
.
Let
\{e_{1}^{*},\ldots,e_{r}^{*}\}
denote the dual basis of
\{e_{1},\ldots,e_{r}\}
.
Then the element
x\in{\bigcap}^{r}_{R}\mathbf{R}^{1}\Gamma_{\fin}(G_{\mathbb{Q},\Sigma},T,\Delta_{\mathscr{F}^{+}})
defined by
x(e_{1}^{*}\wedge\cdots\wedge e_{r}^{*})=1
is a basis and the element
y\in{\bigcap}^{s}_{R}\mathbf{R}^{1}\Gamma_{\fin}(G_{\mathbb{Q},\Sigma},T,\Delta_{\mathscr{F}})
defined by
y(e_{1}^{*}\wedge\cdots\wedge e_{s}^{*})=1
is a basis.
Then

\Phi(x)(e_{1}^{*}\wedge\cdots\wedge e_{s}^{*})=x(e_{1}^{*}\wedge\cdots\wedge e_{s}^{*}\wedge(\varphi_{1}\circ\mathrm{res}_{p})\wedge\cdots\wedge(\varphi_{r-s}\circ\mathrm{res}_{p}))=\pi^{\sum a_{i}}=\pi^{\ell_{F}-\ell_{F^{+}}}.

Hence
\Phi(x)=\pi^{\ell_{F}-\ell_{F^{+}}}y
and Theorem
4.3
(ii) shows that

\Phi(\delta(T,\Delta_{\mathscr{F}^{+}}))=\Phi(R\pi^{\ell_{F^{+}}}x)=R\pi^{\ell_{F}}y=\delta(T,\Delta_{\mathscr{F}}).

∎

Lemma 4.5
.

Suppose that
R
is normal.
Let
M
be a torsion-free
R
-module and let
f,g\in M
.
Assume that for every height-one prime
\mathfrak{p}
of
R
, there exists a unit
u_{\mathfrak{p}}\in R_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\times}
such that
f=u_{\mathfrak{p}}g
in
M_{\mathfrak{p}}
.
Then there exists a unit
u\in R^{\times}
such that
f=ug
.

Proof.

Since there is an injective homomorphism from
Rf+Rg
into
R
, we may assume that
M=R
.
If we consider
R
and each
R_{\mathfrak{p}}
as subrings of the field
\mathrm{Frac}(R)
, the assumption implies that

\frac{f}{g},\,\frac{g}{f}\in\bigcap_{\mathrm{ht}(\mathfrak{p})=1}R_{\mathfrak{p}}\,.

As
R
is normal, we have
\bigcap_{\mathrm{ht}(\mathfrak{p})=1}R_{\mathfrak{p}}=R
, which shows that
f/g\in R^{\times}
.
∎

Theorem 4.6
.

Suppose that
R
is normal.
Then

\Phi(\delta(T,\Delta_{\mathscr{F}^{+}}))=\delta(T,\Delta_{\mathscr{F}}).

Proof.

By Lemma
4.4
and Theorem
4.3
(ii), we have

\Phi(\delta(T,\Delta_{\mathscr{F}^{+}}))R_{\mathfrak{p}}=\delta(T,\Delta_{\mathscr{F}})R_{\mathfrak{p}}

for every height-one prime
\mathfrak{p}
of
R
.
Since both
\Phi(\delta(T,\Delta_{\mathscr{F}^{+}}))
and
\delta(T,\Delta_{\mathscr{F}})
are cyclic
R
-modules, we may apply Lemma
4.5
with

M=\bigcap\nolimits_{R}^{s}\mathbf{R}^{1}\Gamma_{\fin}(G_{\mathbb{Q},\Sigma},T,\Delta_{\mathscr{F}}),

taking
f
and
g
to be generators of
\Phi(\delta(T,\Delta_{\mathscr{F}^{+}}))
and
\delta(T,\Delta_{\mathscr{F}})
, respectively.
It then follows that

\Phi(\delta(T,\Delta_{\mathscr{F}^{+}}))=\delta(T,\Delta_{\mathscr{F}}).

∎

4.2.2.
Perrin-Riou’s large exponential map

Following closely the discussion in
[
BS25
]
, we introduce Perrin-Riou’s large logarithm maps. These, together with the input from §
3.7
, will be used to formulate our factorisation conjecture (and also to trivialise naturally various local cohomology groups, e.g. in place of
\varphi_{i}
in §
4.2.1
above).

As in previous sections, let
R
be a complete local Noetherian domain and let
X
be a free
R
-module of rank one, which is equipped with a continuous
G_{p}
-action. Let us assume that
X=X^{\circ}\otimes\eta
where the
G_{p}
-representation
X^{\circ}
is unramified and
\eta
is a character of
\Gamma_{\cyc}
. We consider the following condition on
X
:

NA)

H^{2}(G_{p},X)=0
.

We remark that, by local duality, this condition is equivalent to the requirement that
H^{0}(G_{p},\overline{X}^{*}(1))=0
, where
\overline{X}
is the residual representation.

The theory of Coleman maps gives rise to an isomorphism (cf.
[
KLZ17b
]
, §8.2)

(4.1)

{\rm LOG}_{X^{\circ},0}\,:\,H^{1}(G_{p},X^{\circ}\widehat{\otimes}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{Z}_{p}[[\Gamma_{\cyc}]])\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\mathbf{D}}_{\rm cris}(X^{\circ})\,\widehat{\otimes}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\,\Lambda(\Gamma_{\cyc})\,,

where
{\mathbf{D}}_{\rm cris}(X^{\circ})=(X^{\circ}\,\widehat{\otimes}\,\widehat{\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{u}r}})^{G_{p}}
. Let us define the morphism

(4.2)

{\rm Log}_{X}\,:\,H^{1}(G_{p},X)\longrightarrow{\mathbf{D}}_{\rm cris}(X^{\circ})

on tensoring the isomorphism (
4.1
) of
R[[\Gamma_{\cyc}]]
-modules by
R[[\Gamma_{\cyc}]]\big/(\gamma-1)
(where
\gamma\in\Gamma_{\cyc}
is a topological generator) and relying on the fact that the natural injection

H^{1}(G_{p},X\widehat{\otimes}_{R}R[[\Gamma_{\cyc}]])\Big/(\gamma-1)\longrightarrow H^{1}(G_{p},X)

is an isomorphism whenever (
NA)
) holds, in which case the map
{\rm Log}_{X}
is an isomorphism.

Leading terms: Arithmetic examples

We apply the general formalism of the preceding subsection in the context of Galois representations attached to forms on
{\rm GSp}_{4}\times{\rm GL}_{2}\times{\rm GL}_{2}
, and their subquotients.

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} {\rm Log}_{\eta_{i}}\,&:\,H^{1}(G_{p},{\mathscr{G}r}^{i}T^{\dagger}_{\underline{\Pi}})\xrightarrow[{\rm Log}_{{\mathscr{G}r}^{i}T^{\dagger}_{\underline{\Pi}}}]{\sim}{\mathbf{D}}_{\rm cris}({\mathscr{G}r}^{i}T^{\dagger}_{\underline{\Pi}}{}^{\circ})\xrightarrow[\,\eta_{i}\,]{\sim}{\mathcal{R}}_{{\underline{\Pi}}}\,,\\

of such

5.
Factorisation of algebraic
p
-adic
L
-functions

In this section, we establish the factorisations of modules of leading terms. We will carry this out for regions (d) and (a) separately, as these two cases exhibit fundamental differences (cf. §
1.5
).

We assume throughout this section, without loss of generality
15
15

15

i.e. on base-changing to its normal closure.
, that
{\mathcal{R}}_{3}
is normal.

5.1.
Wall-crossing: (d) to (e)

Unlike the case “(a) to (b)” (which involves new phenomena), this case can be handled following the ideas of
[
BS25
]
.

We note that our main results in this subsection are guided by the (BDP) “wall-crossing principle” (see §
1.5
): The restriction of the (conjectural)
p
-adic
L
-function for the region (d) to
{\rm im}({\rm Spec}\,{\mathcal{R}}_{3}\xrightarrow{\iota_{3,4}}{\rm Spec}\,{\mathcal{R}}_{4})
has empty interpolation range. Moreover,
{\rm im}(\iota_{3,4})
contains a dense set of classical points that fall within the region (e) (where the global root number equals
-1
), and the values of the (conjectural)
p
-adic
L
-function for the region (d) at such points in the region (e) should be a
p
-adic avatar for the first derivative of the degree–16
L
-function at the central critical point.

Theorem 5.1
.

Suppose that
\delta(T_{3}^{{\dagger}},\Delta_{d})\neq 0=\delta(T^{\dagger}_{\underline{\Pi}},\Delta_{\mathscr{F}^{2}})
. Then,

\delta(T_{3}^{{\dagger}},\Delta_{d})=\Log_{d/e}(\delta(M_{3}^{{\dagger}},{\mathrm{tr}}^{*}\Delta_{d}))\cdot\varpi_{3,2}^{*}\Log_{c}(\delta(T^{\dagger}_{\underline{\Pi}},\Delta_{\mathscr{F}^{1}})).

In particular,
\delta(M_{3}^{{\dagger}},\Delta_{e})=\Log_{d/e}(\delta(M_{3}^{{\dagger}},{\mathrm{tr}}^{*}\Delta_{d}))\neq 0
.

We will complete the proof of this theorem after some preparatory steps. Before we proceed, let us briefly comment on the hypotheses of this theorem. If we had
16
16

16

Folklore conjectures imply that this case should never arise.

\delta(T_{3}^{{\dagger}},\Delta_{d})=0
, then it is easy to see that the claimed factorisation in Theorem
5.1
reduces to the uninteresting assertion that
0=0
. The required vanishing
\delta(T^{\dagger}_{\underline{\Pi}},\Delta_{\mathscr{F}^{2}})=0
under our running assumption on global
\varepsilon
-factors is a consequence of the parity conjecture for
{\rm GSp}_{4}
.

Lemma 5.2
.

We have the following commutative diagram with exact rows:

\textstyle{\mathbf{R}\Gamma_{\fin}(G_{\mathbb{Q},\Sigma},T_{{\underline{\Pi}}}^{\dagger},\Delta_{\mathscr{F}^{3}})\otimes_{\varpi_{3,2}^{*}}{\mathcal{R}}_{3}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{=}

\scriptstyle{{\rm tr}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\rm tr}

\scriptstyle{+1}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{res}_{p}}

Here, we have use the shorthand

for any

.

Lemma 5.3
.

Suppose that
\delta(T_{3}^{{\dagger}},\Delta_{d})\neq 0
.

\mathbf{R}^{1}\Gamma_{\fin}(G_{\mathbb{Q},\Sigma},T^{\dagger}_{\underline{\Pi}},\Delta_{\mathscr{F}^{3}})=0=\mathbf{R}^{1}\Gamma_{\fin}(G_{\mathbb{Q},\Sigma},T_{3}^{\dagger},\Delta_{d})
.

We have

\textstyle{\mathbf{R}^{1}\Gamma_{\fin}(G_{\mathbb{Q},\Sigma},M_{3}^{\dagger},\mathrm{tr}^{*}\Delta_{d})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta^{1}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{res}_{p}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{R}^{2}\Gamma_{\fin}(G_{\mathbb{Q},\Sigma},T_{{\underline{\Pi}}}^{\dagger},\Delta_{\mathscr{F}^{3}})\otimes_{\varpi_{3,2}^{*}}{\mathcal{R}}_{3}.}
\textstyle{\dfrac{{H}^{1}\left(G_{p},{\mathscr{F}^{1}}/{\mathscr{F}^{3}}\right)}{\mathrm{res}_{p}({\mathbf{R}^{1}\Gamma_{\fin}(G_{\mathbb{Q},\Sigma},T_{{\underline{\Pi}}}^{\dagger},\Delta_{\mathscr{F}^{1}}))}}\otimes_{\varpi_{3,2}^{*}}{\mathcal{R}}_{3}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial_{\underline{\Pi}}}

All the
{\mathcal{R}}_{3}
-modules that appear in this diagram are of rank one.

\delta(T_{3}^{{\dagger}},\Delta_{d})=\Char_{{\mathcal{R}}_{3}}\left(\dfrac{\mathbf{R}^{2}\Gamma_{\fin}(G_{\mathbb{Q},\Sigma},T_{{\underline{\Pi}}}^{\dagger},\Delta_{\mathscr{F}^{3}})\otimes_{\varpi_{3,2}^{*}}{\mathcal{R}}_{3}}{\delta^{1}(\delta(M_{3}^{{\dagger}},\mathrm{tr}^{*}\Delta_{d}))}\right)
.

Proof.

Since

, we have

\mathbf{R}^{1}\Gamma_{\fin}(G_{\mathbb{Q},\Sigma},T_{3}^{\dagger},\Delta_{d})=0\,,

and the vanishing

follows from the first row of the diagram in Lemma

. This concludes the proof of (i). Utilising the same lemma again, we obtain the following commutative diagram with exact rows:

\textstyle{\mathbf{R}^{1}\Gamma_{\fin}(G_{\mathbb{Q},\Sigma},T_{3}^{\dagger},\Delta_{d})=0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

The proof of (ii) follows immediately chasing this diagram. Part (iii) follows from the exactness of the sequence

\displaystyle 0\longrightarrow

\displaystyle\,\frac{\mathbf{R}^{1}\Gamma_{\fin}(G_{\mathbb{Q},\Sigma},M_{3}^{\dagger},\mathrm{tr}^{*}\Delta_{d})}{\delta(M_{3}^{{\dagger}},\mathrm{tr}^{*}\Delta_{d})}\xrightarrow{\delta^{1}}\frac{\mathbf{R}^{2}\Gamma_{\fin}(G_{\mathbb{Q},\Sigma},T_{{\underline{\Pi}}}^{\dagger},\Delta_{\mathscr{F}^{3}})\otimes_{\varpi_{3,2}^{*}}{\mathcal{R}}_{3}}{\delta^{1}(\delta(M_{3}^{{\dagger}},\mathrm{tr}^{*}\Delta_{d}))}

Lemma 5.4
.

Suppose that
\delta(T_{3}^{{\dagger}},\Delta_{d})\neq 0
. Then:

\displaystyle\Char_{{\mathcal{R}}_{3}}\left(\frac{\mathbf{R}^{2}\Gamma_{\fin}(G_{\mathbb{Q},\Sigma},T_{{\underline{\Pi}}}^{\dagger},\Delta_{\mathscr{F}^{3}})\otimes_{\varpi_{3,2}^{*}}{\mathcal{R}}_{3}}{\delta^{1}(\delta(M_{3}^{{\dagger}},\mathrm{tr}^{*}\Delta_{d}))}\right)=

\displaystyle\Char_{{\mathcal{R}}_{3}}\left(\frac{\dfrac{H^{1}(G_{p},\mathscr{F}^{1}/\mathscr{F}^{3})}{\mathrm{res}_{p}(\mathbf{R}^{1}\Gamma_{\fin}(G_{\mathbb{Q},\Sigma},T_{{\underline{\Pi}}}^{\dagger},\Delta_{\mathscr{F}^{1}}))}\otimes_{\varpi_{3,2}^{*}}{\mathcal{R}}_{3}}{\mathrm{res}_{p}(\delta(M_{3}^{{\dagger}},\mathrm{tr}^{*}\Delta_{d}))}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times\,\varpi^{*}_{3,2}\Char_{{\mathcal{R}}_{\underline{\Pi}}}\left(\mathbf{R}^{2}\Gamma_{\fin}(G_{\mathbb{Q},\Sigma},T_{{\underline{\Pi}}}^{\dagger},\Delta_{\mathscr{F}^{1}})\right).

Proof.

This is an immediate consequence of the exact sequence

\displaystyle 0\longrightarrow

\displaystyle\,\frac{\dfrac{H^{1}(G_{p},\mathscr{F}^{1}/\mathscr{F}^{3})}{\mathrm{res}_{p}(\mathbf{R}^{1}\Gamma_{\fin}(G_{\mathbb{Q},\Sigma},T_{{\underline{\Pi}}}^{\dagger},\Delta_{\mathscr{F}^{1}}))}\otimes_{\varpi_{3,2}^{*}}{\mathcal{R}}_{3}}{\mathrm{res}_{p}(\delta(M_{3}^{{\dagger}},\mathrm{tr}^{*}\Delta_{d}))}\longrightarrow\frac{\mathbf{R}^{2}\Gamma_{\fin}(G_{\mathbb{Q},\Sigma},T_{{\underline{\Pi}}}^{\dagger},\Delta_{\mathscr{F}^{3}})\otimes_{\varpi_{3,2}^{*}}{\mathcal{R}}_{3}}{\delta^{1}(\delta(M_{3}^{{\dagger}},\mathrm{tr}^{*}\Delta_{d}))}

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt\longrightarrow\mathbf{R}^{2}\Gamma_{\fin}(G_{\mathbb{Q},\Sigma},T_{{\underline{\Pi}}}^{\dagger},\Delta_{\mathscr{F}^{1}})\otimes_{\varpi_{3,2}^{*}}{\mathcal{R}}_{3}\longrightarrow H^{2}(G_{p},\mathscr{F}^{1}/\mathscr{F}^{3}\otimes_{\varpi_{3,2}^{*}}{\mathcal{R}}_{3})=0

obtained from the diagram in Lemma
5.2
.
∎

Lemma 5.5
.

Suppose that
\delta(T_{3}^{{\dagger}},\Delta_{d})\neq 0=\delta(T^{\dagger}_{\underline{\Pi}},\Delta_{\mathscr{F}^{2}})
. Then:

\mathbf{R}^{1}\Gamma_{\fin}(G_{\mathbb{Q},\Sigma},T_{{\underline{\Pi}}}^{\dagger},\Delta_{\mathscr{F}^{1}})=\mathbf{R}^{1}\Gamma_{\fin}(G_{\mathbb{Q},\Sigma},T_{{\underline{\Pi}}}^{\dagger},\Delta_{\mathscr{F}^{2}})\,.

Proof.

Recall that
\mathbf{R}^{1}\Gamma_{\fin}(G_{\mathbb{Q},\Sigma},T_{{\underline{\Pi}}}^{\dagger},\Delta_{\mathscr{F}^{3}})=0
under our running hypotheses.
It follows from global duality combined with the fact that
\chi(\mathbf{R}\Gamma_{\fin}(G_{\mathbb{Q},\Sigma},T_{{\underline{\Pi}}}^{\dagger},\Delta_{\mathscr{F}^{1}}))=1
and the vanishing above that

\mathrm{rank}_{{\mathcal{R}}_{\underline{\Pi}}}\mathbf{R}^{1}\Gamma_{\fin}(G_{\mathbb{Q},\Sigma},T_{{\underline{\Pi}}}^{\dagger},\Delta_{\mathscr{F}^{1}})=1.

This shows that
\mathrm{rank}_{{\mathcal{R}}_{\underline{\Pi}}}\mathbf{R}^{1}\Gamma_{\fin}(G_{\mathbb{Q},\Sigma},T_{{\underline{\Pi}}}^{\dagger},\Delta_{\mathscr{F}^{2}})\leq 1
. Moreover, our running assumption that
\delta(T^{\dagger}_{\underline{\Pi}},\Delta_{\mathscr{F}^{2}})=0
implies that
\mathbf{R}^{1}\Gamma_{\fin}(G_{\mathbb{Q},\Sigma},T_{{\underline{\Pi}}}^{\dagger},\Delta_{\mathscr{F}^{2}})
has positive rank, which in turn shows that

\mathrm{rank}_{{\mathcal{R}}_{\underline{\Pi}}}\mathbf{R}^{1}\Gamma_{\fin}(G_{\mathbb{Q},\Sigma},T_{{\underline{\Pi}}}^{\dagger},\Delta_{\mathscr{F}^{1}})=1=\mathrm{rank}_{{\mathcal{R}}_{\underline{\Pi}}}\mathbf{R}^{1}\Gamma_{\fin}(G_{\mathbb{Q},\Sigma},T_{{\underline{\Pi}}}^{\dagger},\Delta_{\mathscr{F}^{2}})\,.

Finally, the quotient
\mathbf{R}^{1}\Gamma_{\fin}(G_{\mathbb{Q},\Sigma},T_{{\underline{\Pi}}}^{\dagger},\Delta_{\mathscr{F}^{1}})/\mathbf{R}^{1}\Gamma_{\fin}(G_{\mathbb{Q},\Sigma},T_{{\underline{\Pi}}}^{\dagger},\Delta_{\mathscr{F}^{2}})
is torsion-free since it is canonically isomorphic to a submodule of
H^{1}(G_{p},\mathscr{F}^{1}/\mathscr{F}^{2})
. Our lemma follows on combining these facts.
∎

Lemma 5.6
.

Suppose that
\delta(T_{3}^{{\dagger}},\Delta_{d})\neq 0=\delta(T^{\dagger}_{\underline{\Pi}},\Delta_{\mathscr{F}^{2}})
. Then:

\displaystyle\Char_{{\mathcal{R}}_{3}}\left(\frac{\mathbf{R}^{2}\Gamma_{\fin}(G_{\mathbb{Q},\Sigma},T_{{\underline{\Pi}}}^{\dagger},\Delta_{\mathscr{F}^{3}})\otimes_{\varpi_{3,2}^{*}}{\mathcal{R}}_{3}}{\delta^{1}(\delta(M_{3}^{{\dagger}},\mathrm{tr}^{*}\Delta_{d}))}\right)=\varpi^{*}_{3,2}\Log_{c}(\delta(T_{{\underline{\Pi}}}^{\dagger},\Delta_{\mathscr{F}^{1}}))\Log_{d/e}(\delta(M_{3}^{\dagger},\mathrm{tr}^{*}\Delta_{d})).

Proof.

Since
\mathbf{R}^{1}\Gamma_{\fin}(G_{\mathbb{Q},\Sigma},T_{{\underline{\Pi}}}^{\dagger},\Delta_{\mathscr{F}^{1}})=\mathbf{R}^{1}\Gamma_{\fin}(G_{\mathbb{Q},\Sigma},T_{{\underline{\Pi}}}^{\dagger},\Delta_{\mathscr{F}^{2}})
by the previous lemma, we have an exact sequence

(5.1)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} 0\rightarrow\dfrac{H^{1}(G_{p},\mathscr{F}^{2}/\mathscr{F}^{3})}{\mathrm{res}_{p}(\mathbf{R}^{1}\Gamma_{\fin}(G_{\mathbb{Q},\Sigma},T_{{\underline{\Pi}}}^{\dagger},\Delta_{\mathscr{F}^{1}}))}\longrightarrow\dfrac{H^{1}(G_{p},\mathscr{F}^{1}/\mathscr{F}^{3})}{\mathrm{res}_{p}(\mathbf{R}^{1}\Gamma_{\fin}(G_{\mathbb{Q},\Sigma},T_{{\underline{\Pi}}}^{\dagger},\Delta_{\mathscr{F}^{1}}))}\longrightarrow H^{1}(G_{p},\mathscr{F}^{1}/\mathscr{F}^{2})\rightarrow 0.\end{aligned}

Relying on the fact that the left-most term in (
5.1
) is torsion (so that the intersection of
\mathrm{res}_{p}(\delta(M_{3}^{{\dagger}},\mathrm{tr}^{*}\Delta_{d}))
with this module is trivial), we therefore have

(5.2)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \Char_{{\mathcal{R}}_{3}}&\left(\frac{\dfrac{H^{1}(G_{p},\mathscr{F}^{1}/\mathscr{F}^{3})}{\mathrm{res}_{p}(\mathbf{R}^{1}\Gamma_{\fin}(G_{\mathbb{Q},\Sigma},T_{{\underline{\Pi}}}^{\dagger},\Delta_{\mathscr{F}^{1}}))}\otimes_{\varpi_{3,2}^{*}}{\mathcal{R}}_{3}}{\mathrm{res}_{p}(\delta(M_{3}^{{\dagger}},\mathrm{tr}^{*}\Delta_{d}))}\right)\\
&\hskip 71.13188pt=\varpi_{3,2}^{*}\Char_{{\mathcal{R}}_{\underline{\Pi}}}\left(\dfrac{H^{1}(G_{p},\mathscr{F}^{2}/\mathscr{F}^{3})}{\mathrm{res}_{p}(\mathbf{R}^{1}\Gamma_{\fin}(G_{\mathbb{Q},\Sigma},T_{{\underline{\Pi}}}^{\dagger},\Delta_{\mathscr{F}^{1}})}\right)\times\Log_{d/e}(\delta(M_{3}^{\dagger},\mathrm{tr}^{*}\Delta_{d})).\end{aligned}

Finally, by Theorem
4.3
(ii), we have

\Char_{{\mathcal{R}}_{\underline{\Pi}}}\left(\mathbf{R}^{2}\Gamma_{\fin}(G_{\mathbb{Q},\Sigma},T_{{\underline{\Pi}}}^{\dagger},\Delta_{\mathscr{F}^{1}})\right)=\Char_{{\mathcal{R}}_{\underline{\Pi}}}\left(\mathbf{R}^{1}\Gamma_{\fin}(G_{\mathbb{Q},\Sigma},T_{{\underline{\Pi}}}^{\dagger},\Delta_{\mathscr{F}^{1}})/\delta(T_{{\underline{\Pi}}}^{\dagger},\Delta_{\mathscr{F}^{1}})\right)

Since
\mathrm{res}_{p}
is injective, we conclude that

(5.3)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \Char_{{\mathcal{R}}_{\underline{\Pi}}}\left(\dfrac{H^{1}(G_{p},\mathscr{F}^{2}/\mathscr{F}^{3})}{\mathrm{res}_{p}(\mathbf{R}^{1}\Gamma_{\fin}(G_{\mathbb{Q},\Sigma},T_{{\underline{\Pi}}}^{\dagger},\Delta_{\mathscr{F}^{1}})}\right)&\,\Char_{{\mathcal{R}}_{\underline{\Pi}}}\left(\mathbf{R}^{2}\Gamma_{\fin}(G_{\mathbb{Q},\Sigma},T_{{\underline{\Pi}}}^{\dagger},\Delta_{\mathscr{F}^{1}})\right)\\
&\hskip 56.9055pt=\Char_{{\mathcal{R}}_{\underline{\Pi}}}\left(\dfrac{H^{1}(G_{p},\mathscr{F}^{2}/\mathscr{F}^{3})}{\mathrm{res}_{p}(\delta(T_{{\underline{\Pi}}}^{\dagger},\Delta_{\mathscr{F}^{1}}))}\right)\\
&\hskip 56.9055pt=\Log_{c}(\delta(T_{{\underline{\Pi}}}^{\dagger},\Delta_{\mathscr{F}^{1}})).\end{aligned}

The proof of our lemma follows on combining (
5.2
) and (
5.3
) with Lemma
5.4
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
5.1
.

The asserted factorisation follows from Lemma
5.3
(iii) and Lemma
5.6
.
∎

5.2.
Double Wall-crossing: (a) to (c)

As in the previous section, the wall-crossing principle serves as our signpost. We summarize the portion of this philosophy relevant to our setting before stating our main result in this section.

The restriction of the (conjectural)
p
-adic
L
-function for the region (a) to
{\rm im}({\rm Spec}\,{\mathcal{R}}_{3}\xrightarrow{\iota_{3,4}}{\rm Spec}\,{\mathcal{R}}_{4})
has empty interpolation range. Moreover,
{\rm im}(\iota_{3,4})
contains a dense set of classical points that fall within the region (b) (where the global root number equals
-1
). However, as a key difference with the case considered in §
5.1
, the degree–12 motives associated with these specialisations that belong to the region (b) are no longer critical. The correct interpretation, therefore, is that the values of the (conjectural)
p
-adic
L
-function for the region (a) at classical points in the region (c) should be a
p
-adic avatar for the second-order central critical derivative of the complex
L
-series associated to degree–16 motives with weights in region (c). Note that the global sign for the degree–16 motives with weights in region (c) equals +1, whereas the same for the degree–12 and degree–4 motives is
-1
.

Our main result in this subsection is the factorisation statement in Theorem
5.13
, which reflects this principle: The first factor on the right is a
p
-adic avatar of the central critical derivatives of the complex
L
-series associated with degree–12 motives with weights in the region (c), whereas the second factor is the same for the degree–4 motives.

5.2.1.
Hypotheses

Before we state this result, we record the hypotheses that we will rely on in due course:

\mathbf{AJ}_{p}^{{\underline{\Pi}}}
)

\delta(T^{\dagger}_{\underline{\Pi}},\Delta_{\mathscr{F}^{1}})\neq 0
.

\mathbf{P}^{-}_{{\underline{\Pi}}}
)

\delta(T^{\dagger}_{\underline{\Pi}},\Delta_{\mathscr{F}^{2}})=0
.

\mathbf{P}^{-}_{M}
)

\delta(M_{3}^{\dagger},{\rm tr}^{*}\Delta_{c})=0
.

\mathbf{AJ}_{p}^{M}
)

\delta(M_{3}^{\dagger},\Delta_{b})\neq 0
.

\mathbf{nV}_{T}^{a}
)

\delta(T_{3}^{{\dagger}},\Delta_{a})\neq 0
.

Lemma 5.7
.

If the parity conjecture for classical specialisations of
T_{\underline{\Pi}}^{\dagger}
holds true, then so does (

P
-

¯
Π

)
). Consequently,
\delta(T_{\underline{\Pi}}^{\dagger},\Delta_{\mathscr{F}^{1}})\in\mathbf{R}^{1}\Gamma_{\fin}(G_{\mathbb{Q},\Sigma},T_{\underline{\Pi}}^{\dagger},\Delta_{\mathscr{F}^{2}})
.

Proof.

Since the global root number of the classical specialisations of
T_{\underline{\Pi}}^{\dagger}
is equal to
-1
, it follows that the rank of
\mathbf{R}^{2}\Gamma_{\fin}(G_{\mathbb{Q},\Sigma},T_{\underline{\Pi}}^{\dagger},\Delta_{\mathscr{F}^{2}})
is odd. In particular, it cannot be torsion, and the conclusion follows from Theorem
4.3
(i). The second conclusion follows from Theorem

, which tells us that

∎

5.2.2.

We are now ready to state one of our main results. This is Theorem
5.13
below; see also Theorem
5.29
for an important intermediate step: the factorisation of
\delta(T_{3}^{{\dagger}},\Delta_{a})
, echoing the double-wall-crossing principle (from the region
a
to region
c
). We recall that we work under Hypotheses
5.2.1
until the end of §
5.2
, unless we explicitly state otherwise.

As the final preparatory step, we set

S(M,{\underline{\Pi}}):=d_{\underline{\Pi}}^{-2}d_{M}^{-2}(c_{M}d_{\underline{\Pi}}-c_{\underline{\Pi}}d_{M})^{2},

where the quantities
c_{M},d_{M},c_{\underline{\Pi}},d_{\underline{\Pi}}
are defined in §
5.2.7
–§
5.2.9
. We roughly note that
S(M,{\underline{\Pi}})
measures the relative positions of the Selmer groups for the families
M_{3}^{\dagger}
and
T_{\underline{\Pi}}^{\dagger}
(where the modules of leading terms associated to these live, as well as the relevant Abel–Jacobi images of the conjectural cycles) with respect to the splittings determined by
{\rm tr}
versus
{\rm tr}^{*}
; cf. the cartesian square
(
A) in (
5.4
) and
(
B) in (
5.8
). We finally remark that such a fudge factor is relevant to the double-wall-crossing scenario as opposed to the single-wall-crossing, as a reflection of the fact that (conjectural) cycles associated with both factors
M_{3}^{\dagger}
and
T_{\underline{\Pi}}^{\dagger}
are required to explain the arithmetic meaning of
L_{p}^{(a)}{}_{|_{(c)}}
(hence also a comparison of the ambient spaces where these live in), whereas for
L_{p}^{(d)}{}_{|_{(e)}}
(single-wall-crossing scenario), only the (conjectural) family of cycles associated with
T_{\underline{\Pi}}^{\dagger}
are required for the same purpose.

Theorem 5.13
.

Assume that Hypotheses
5.2.1
hold. Then,

\displaystyle\delta(T_{3}^{{\dagger}},\Delta_{a})

\displaystyle=S(M,{\underline{\Pi}})\cdot{\rm Log}_{c}\wedge{\rm Log}_{a/b}(\delta(M_{3}^{{\dagger}},{\mathrm{tr}}^{*}\Delta_{a}))\cdot\varpi_{3,2}^{*}\,\circ\,\Log_{c}\wedge\Log_{a/b}(\delta(T^{\dagger}_{\underline{\Pi}},\Delta_{\emptyset}))

\displaystyle=S(M,{\underline{\Pi}})\cdot{\rm Log}_{c}\delta(M_{3}^{{\dagger}},{\mathrm{tr}}^{*}\Delta_{b})\cdot\varpi_{3,2}^{*}\,\circ\,\Log_{c}(\delta(T^{\dagger}_{\underline{\Pi}},\Delta_{\mathscr{F}^{1}}))\,.

The proof of Theorem
5.13
will occupy the remainder of Section
5.2
.

Lemma 5.14
.

Let
S
be a regular ring, and let
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
be torsion-free
S
-modules of rank
r
. Suppose that
f\colon X_{1}\to X_{2}
be an injective
S
-homomorphism. Then for any
\delta\in{\bigcap}^{r}_{S}\,X_{1}
, we have

\Char_{S}\left(\left.{\bigcap}^{r}_{S}\,X_{2}\middle/Sf^{(r)}(\delta)\right.\right)=\Char_{S}\left(\left.{\bigcap}^{r}_{S}\,X_{1}\middle/S\delta\right.\right)\Char_{S}\left(\coker(f)\right).

Here
f^{(r)}\colon{\bigcap}^{r}_{S}\,X_{1}\longrightarrow{\bigcap}^{r}_{S}\,X_{2}

denotes the
S
-homomorphism induced by
f
.

Proof.

On localizing the ring
S
at a height-one prime, we may assume that
S
is a discrete valuation ring. Then
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
are free
S
-module of rank
r
, and hence we may also assume that
X_{1}=X_{2}=S^{r}
. In this case, this lemma follows from the fact that
{\bigcap}^{r}_{S}\,S^{r}=S
combined with the fact that
f^{(r)}\colon S\to S
is given by
x\mapsto\det(f)x
.
∎

Definition 5.15
.

Given an integral domain
S
and an
S
-module
X
, let us denote by
X_{\rm tor}\subset X
the torsion-submodule of
X
and denote by
X_{\rm tf}:=X/X_{\rm tor}
its maximal torsion-free quotient.
\blacksquare
