, shows that

\displaystyle\Log^{(2)}_{1}(\delta(M_{3,1}^{\dagger},{\mathrm{tr}}^{*}\Delta_{\underline{\sigma}})\otimes\delta(T_{{\underline{\mathbf{f}}}_{1}}^{\dagger},\Delta_{\emptyset})_{{\mathcal{R}}_{3}})

\displaystyle=\Log_{\omega_{M_{3,1}}}(\delta(M_{3,1}^{\dagger},{\rm tr}^{*}\Delta_{\underline{\sigma}}))\times\Log_{F^{-}}(\mathrm{res}_{p}(\delta(T^{\dagger}_{{\underline{\mathbf{f}}}_{1}},\Delta_{\emptyset})_{{\mathcal{R}}_{3}}))

\displaystyle=\Log_{\omega_{M_{3,1}}}(\delta(M_{3,1}^{\dagger},{\rm tr}^{*}\Delta_{\underline{\sigma}}))\times\delta(T^{\dagger}_{{\underline{\mathbf{f}}}_{1}},\Delta_{F^{+}})_{{\mathcal{R}}_{3}}.

On the other hand, since
\delta(T^{\dagger}_{{\underline{\mathbf{f}}}_{2}},\Delta_{F^{+}})=0
by the assumption (

P
-

¯
f

2

)
), it follows from Theorem
4.6
that
\Log_{F^{-}}\circ\mathrm{res}_{p}(\delta(T_{{\underline{\mathbf{f}}}_{2}}^{\dagger},\Delta_{\emptyset})_{{\mathcal{R}}_{3}})=0
.
Therefore, by the same argument, we have

\Log^{(2)}_{2}(\delta(M_{3,2}^{\dagger},{\mathrm{tr}}^{*}\Delta_{\underline{\sigma}})\otimes\delta(T_{{\underline{\mathbf{f}}}_{2}}^{\dagger},\Delta_{\emptyset})_{{\mathcal{R}}_{3}})=\delta(M_{3,2}^{\dagger},{\mathrm{tr}}^{*}\Delta_{\rm bal})\times\Log_{\omega_{{\underline{\mathbf{f}}}_{2}}}\delta(T_{{\underline{\mathbf{f}}}_{2}}^{\dagger},\Delta_{\emptyset})_{{\mathcal{R}}_{3}}

(see also
[
BS25
]
, Theorem 6.7).
∎

Remark 6.17
.

Suppose that
\mathscr{F}=\mathscr{F}_{1}^{\bullet}
.
Then by Proposition
6.11
(i), we have

\Log_{\omega_{{\underline{\mathbf{f}}}_{2}}}=\Log_{\rm c}
.
On the other hand, Proposition
6.13
(i) implies that

\Log_{M_{3,1}}=\Log_{\mathscr{F}^{3}}\neq\Log_{\rm c}.

Here
\Log_{\mathscr{F}^{3}}
is the homomorphism induced by the isomorphism
H^{1}(G_{p},\mathscr{F}^{3}T_{\underline{\Pi}}^{\dagger})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\mathcal{R}}_{\underline{\Pi}}
. In other words, in the endoscopic cases, the trivialisations of the modules of the relevant leading terms dwell on Eichler–Shimura isomorphisms in different degrees of cohomology.

In the supplementary case when
\mathscr{F}=\mathscr{F}_{2}^{\bullet}
, the situation is reversed; namely, we have
\Log_{M_{3,1}}=\Log_{\mathrm{c}}
,
and
\Log_{\omega_{\underline{\sigma}_{2}}}=\Log_{\mathscr{F}^{3}}\neq\Log_{\mathrm{c}}
.

\blacksquare

Let us put

\displaystyle\Delta_{M_{3}^{\dagger}}^{(c),{\rm alg}}:=\delta(M_{3,1}^{\dagger},{\mathrm{tr}}^{*}\Delta_{\underline{\sigma}})\times\delta(M_{3,2}^{\dagger},{\mathrm{tr}}^{*}\Delta_{\rm bal})\,,\qquad\Delta_{\underline{\Pi}}^{\dagger,{\rm alg}}:=\delta(T_{{\underline{\mathbf{f}}}_{1}}^{\dagger},\Delta_{F^{+}})_{{\mathcal{R}}_{\underline{\Pi}}}\times\delta(T_{{\underline{\mathbf{f}}}_{2}}^{\dagger},\Delta_{\emptyset})_{{\mathcal{R}}_{\underline{\Pi}}}.

Moreover, we define the homomorphisms
\Log_{\omega_{M_{3}}}
and
\Log_{\omega_{{\underline{\Pi}}}}
according to the choice of the filtration
\mathscr{F}
as follows:

\displaystyle\Log_{\omega_{M_{3}}}:=\begin{cases}\Log_{\mathscr{F}^{3}}&\textrm{ if }\mathscr{F}=\mathscr{F}_{1}^{\bullet},\\
\Log_{c}&\textrm{ if }\mathscr{F}=\mathscr{F}_{2}^{\bullet},\end{cases}\quad\quad\Log_{\omega_{{\underline{\Pi}}}}:=\begin{cases}\Log_{c}&\textrm{ if }\mathscr{F}=\mathscr{F}_{1}^{\bullet},\\
\Log_{\mathscr{F}^{3}}&\textrm{ if }\mathscr{F}=\mathscr{F}_{2}^{\bullet}.\end{cases}

Then, as a corollary of Theorem
6.16
(see also Remark
6.17
), we obtain the following result.

Corollary 6.18
.

In the situation of Theorem
6.16
, we have

\delta(T_{3}^{\dagger},\Delta_{a})=\Log_{\omega_{M_{3}}}(\Delta_{M_{3}^{\dagger}}^{(c),{\rm alg}})\times\Log_{\omega_{{\underline{\Pi}}}}(\Delta_{\underline{\Pi}}^{\dagger,{\rm alg}})\,.

Remark 6.19
.

When
\varepsilon(T_{{\underline{\mathbf{f}}}_{1}}^{\dagger})=-1=-\varepsilon(T_{{\underline{\mathbf{f}}}_{2}}^{\dagger})
, the contents of this subsection remain valid if we exchange the roles of
{\underline{\mathbf{f}}}_{1}
and
{\underline{\mathbf{f}}}_{2}
.

6.3.
Yoshida lifts (bis)

We continue to work in the setting of §
6.1
, but now to supplement §
6.1
to revisit the factorisation problem over the region (d) in this endoscopic scenario and explain that it reduces to
[
BS25
,
BCPdVP25
]
. We have the decompositions (
6.2
), (
6.3
) and (
6.4
) as before. Moreover,

-

region (d) corresponds to the
\underline{\sigma}^{c}
-dominant region for
T_{4,2}^{\dagger}
and
{\underline{\mathbf{f}}}_{1}
-dominant region for
T_{4,1}^{\dagger}
;

-

region (e) corresponds to the balanced region for
T_{4,2}^{\dagger}
and
{\underline{\mathbf{f}}}_{1}
-dominant region for
T_{?,1}^{\dagger}
.

Based on this observation, (
6.2
), and (
6.4
), we set

L_{p}^{(d)}(T_{?}^{\dagger}):=L_{p}^{({\underline{\mathbf{f}}}_{1})}(T_{?,1}^{\dagger})\times L_{p}^{(\underline{\sigma}^{c})}(T_{?,2}^{\dagger})\,,\quad L_{p}^{(d)}(M_{3}^{\dagger}):=L_{p}^{({\underline{\mathbf{f}}}_{1})}(M_{3,1}^{\dagger})\times L_{p}^{\rm bal}(M_{3,2}^{\dagger})\,,\qquad?=3,4\,.

6.3.1.

As we work under the assumption that the global root number of the specialisations of
T_{4}^{\dagger}
in region (e) equals
-1
, we have

\displaystyle-1=\varepsilon^{(e)}(T_{4}^{\dagger})=\varepsilon^{{\underline{\mathbf{f}}}_{1}}(T_{4,1}^{\dagger})\,\cdot\,\varepsilon^{\rm bal}(T_{4,2}^{\dagger})\,=\varepsilon^{{\underline{\mathbf{f}}}_{1}}(M_{3,1})\,\varepsilon(T_{{\underline{\mathbf{f}}}_{1}})\,\cdot\,\varepsilon^{\rm bal}(M_{3,2})\,\varepsilon(T_{{\underline{\mathbf{f}}}_{2}})\,.

That means

-\varepsilon^{{\underline{\mathbf{f}}}_{1}}(T_{4,1}^{\dagger})=\varepsilon^{\rm bal}(T_{4,1}^{\dagger})=\varepsilon^{\rm bal}(T_{4,2}^{\dagger})=\pm 1

and we assume that it is
-1
. In view of (
6.1
), we then have

\varepsilon^{{\underline{\mathbf{f}}}_{1}}(M_{3,1})=1=\varepsilon^{\rm bal}(M_{3,2})\,.

Remark 6.20
.

Unlike in the non-endoscopic cases, classical specialisations of
M_{3}^{\dagger}
in the region (d) are critical in the sense of Deligne: As the discussion above shows, if
\Pi
is a Yoshida lift of
(f_{1},f_{2})
as above, with the propery that
\Pi\times\sigma\times\sigma^{c}
has weights in the region (d), then the
\mathrm{GL}_{2}\times\mathrm{GL}_{3}
-summand
f_{1}\times\ad^{0}\sigma
is
f_{1}
-dominant, whereas
f_{2}\times\ad^{0}\sigma
is balanced.

Theorem 6.21
.

In the setting of the present subsection, let us assume that
[
BCPdVP25
, Conjecture 5.1]
(extension of Gross–Kudla conjecture) as well as (
NA)
) hold true. Then,

(6.10)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} L_{p}^{(d)}(T_{?}^{\dagger})^{2}&:=L_{p}^{({\underline{\mathbf{f}}}_{1})}(T_{3,1}^{\dagger})^{2}\times L_{p}^{(\underline{\sigma}^{c})}(T_{3,2}^{\dagger})^{2}\\
&=\left(L_{p}^{({\underline{\mathbf{f}}}_{1})}(M_{3,1}^{\dagger})\times L_{p}({\underline{\mathbf{f}}}_{1})\right)\times\left(L_{p}^{\rm bal}(M_{3,2}^{\dagger})\times\Log_{\omega_{{\underline{\mathbf{f}}}_{2}}}(\Delta_{{\underline{\mathbf{f}}}_{2}}^{\dagger})^{2}\right)\qquad\pmod{{\mathcal{R}}_{\underline{\Pi}}[1/p]^{\times}}\\
&=\underbrace{\left(L_{p}^{({\underline{\mathbf{f}}}_{1})}(M_{3,1}^{\dagger})\times L_{p}^{\rm bal}(M_{3,2}^{\dagger})\right)}_{L_{p}^{(d)}(M_{3}^{\dagger})}\,\times\,\underbrace{\left(L_{p}({\underline{\mathbf{f}}}_{1})\times\Log_{\omega_{{\underline{\mathbf{f}}}_{2}}}(\Delta_{{\underline{\mathbf{f}}}_{2}}^{\dagger})^{2}\right)}_{\Log_{\omega_{\underline{\Pi}}}(\Delta_{\underline{\Pi}}^{\dagger})^{2}}\qquad\pmod{{\mathcal{R}}_{\underline{\Pi}}[1/p]^{\times}}\,,\end{aligned}

where
\Delta_{{\underline{\mathbf{f}}}_{2}}^{\dagger}
and
\Delta_{\underline{\Pi}}^{\dagger}
are as in the statement of Conjecture
6.3
.

In particular, a slight variant of Conjecture
2.9
holds true in this scenario under the hypothesis of our theorem. In fact, slightly more is true: The ambiguity (which is a factor in
{\mathcal{R}}_{\underline{\Pi}}[1/p]^{\times}
) specialises at classical points to an explicit algebraic number.

Proof.

The second equality follows from a direct comparison of interpolation formulae of
L_{p}^{(f_{1})}(T_{3,1}^{\dagger})
and
L_{p}^{(f_{1})}(M_{3,1}^{\dagger})\times L_{p}({\underline{\mathbf{f}}}_{1})
, combined with the main result of
[
BCPdVP25
]
(Theorem 7.3 combined with Proposition 6.1 in op. cit.).
∎

6.4.
Saito–Kurokawa lifts

In this subsection, we consider the case when
{\underline{\Pi}}
is a (one-parameter) family of Saito–Kurokawa lifts of a Hida family
{\underline{\mathbf{f}}}
of ordinary modular forms. We remark that the assumptions of §
2.3
exclude the case of families of Saito–Kurokawa lifts, and our definition of a family of degree-16
p
-adic
L
-function in this scenario, as in the case of §
6.1
and §
6.3
, is somewhat ad hoc. (cf. §
6.4.7
).

6.4.1.

Let us put
T_{\underline{\Pi}}^{\dagger}=T_{{\underline{\mathbf{f}}}}^{\dagger}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{p}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{p}(1)
, where
{\underline{\mathbf{f}}}
is a Hida family. In this section, we work under the assumption that

\varepsilon({\underline{\mathbf{f}}})=-1=\varepsilon^{\text{bal}}({\underline{\mathbf{f}}}\otimes\underline{\sigma}\otimes\underline{\sigma}^{c})\,,

where
\varepsilon({\underline{\mathbf{f}}})
is the common global root number of the family
{\underline{\mathbf{f}}}
, and
\varepsilon^{\text{bal}}({\underline{\mathbf{f}}}\otimes\underline{\sigma}\otimes\underline{\sigma}^{c})
is the same for the family
{\underline{\mathbf{f}}}\otimes\underline{\sigma}\otimes\underline{\sigma}^{c}
at those weights that are balanced (i.e., the sum of any two of them is greater than the third one).

6.4.2.

The Galois representation
T_{2}^{\dagger}
(resp.
T_{3}^{\dagger}
), which is free of rank
16
over
{\mathcal{R}}_{2}:={\mathcal{R}}_{\underline{\mathbf{f}}}\widehat{\otimes}{\mathcal{R}}_{\underline{\sigma}}
(resp. over
{\mathcal{R}}_{3}:={\mathcal{R}}_{\underline{\mathbf{f}}}\widehat{\otimes}{\mathcal{R}}_{\underline{\sigma}}\widehat{\otimes}{\mathcal{R}}_{\underline{\sigma}}
), admits the following decomposition:

(6.11)

T_{?}^{\dagger}\simeq\underbrace{\left(T_{\underline{\mathbf{f}}}^{\dagger}\widehat{\otimes}T_{\underline{\sigma}}\widehat{\otimes}T_{\underline{\sigma}}^{*}\right)}_{T_{?,1}^{\dagger}}\oplus\underbrace{\left(T_{\underline{\sigma}}\widehat{\otimes}T_{\underline{\sigma}}^{*}\right)\widehat{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{R}}_{\underline{\sigma}}}{\mathcal{R}}_{?}}_{T_{?,2}^{\dagger}}\oplus\underbrace{\left(T_{\underline{\sigma}}\widehat{\otimes}T_{\underline{\sigma}}^{*}(1)\right)\widehat{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{R}}_{\underline{\sigma}}}{\mathcal{R}}_{?}}_{T_{?,3}^{\dagger}}\,,\qquad?=2,3.

If
?=2
, we can further decompose each summand above as

(6.12)

T_{2}^{\dagger}\simeq\underbrace{\left((T_{\underline{\mathbf{f}}}^{\dagger}\,\widehat{\otimes}\,{\rm ad}^{0}T_{\underline{\sigma}})\,\oplus\,(T_{\underline{\mathbf{f}}}^{\dagger}\otimes_{{\mathcal{R}}_{\underline{\mathbf{f}}}}{\mathcal{R}}_{2})\right)}_{T_{2,1}^{\dagger}}\oplus\underbrace{\left({\rm ad}^{0}T_{\underline{\sigma}}\oplus\mathbb{Q}_{p}\right)\otimes_{{\mathcal{R}}_{\underline{\sigma}}}{\mathcal{R}}_{2}}_{T_{2,2}^{\dagger}}\oplus\underbrace{\left({\rm ad}^{0}T_{\underline{\sigma}}(1)\oplus\mathbb{Q}_{p}(1)\right)\otimes_{{\mathcal{R}}_{\underline{\sigma}}}{\mathcal{R}}_{2}}_{T_{2,3}^{\dagger}}\,.

Observe that the degree–4 summands
T^{\dagger}_{?,2}
and
T^{\dagger}_{?,3}
are independent of variation in
{\underline{\mathbf{f}}}
.

6.4.3.

We remark that:

-

Region (a) corresponds to the
\underline{\sigma}^{c}
-dominant region for
T_{3,1}^{\dagger}
. We note that we have
k_{1}=k=k_{2}
in the notation of §
2.5.1
.

-

Region (b) is given by the condition
2\leq c_{2}-c_{1}\leq 2k-4
(since
k_{1}=k_{2}
), together with
2k\leq c_{1}+c_{2}
. This is equivalent to
c_{1}<c_{2}<c_{1}+(2k-2)
, and
2k-2<c_{1}+c_{2}
; hence (since
k\geq 2
), it corresponds to the balanced region for
T_{3,1}^{\dagger}
.

-

Region (c) in this case contains only points with
c_{1}=c_{2}
(since
k_{1}=k_{2}
). In this situation, setting
c:=c_{1}=c_{2}
, the region is defined by the constraint
c\geq k
, and therefore coincides with the balanced region for
T_{3,1}^{\dagger}
.

6.4.4.

Let
L_{p}({\rm ad}^{0}\underline{\sigma})(\lambda,s)
denote the adjoint
p
-adic
L
-function in two variables, constructed by Schmidt
[
Sch88
]
and Hida
[
Hid90
]
, where
s
stands for the cyclotomic variable and
\lambda
for the parameter of the Hida family
\underline{\sigma}
. In view of
[
Das16
, Theorem 2]
, we have

(6.13)

L_{p}(\underline{\sigma}\otimes\underline{\sigma}^{c})(\lambda,\lambda,\wt(\lambda))=L_{p}^{\prime}({\rm ad}^{0}\underline{\sigma})(\lambda,1)\times\mathrm{res}_{s=1}\zeta_{p}(s)=L_{p}^{\prime}({\rm ad}^{0}\underline{\sigma})(\lambda,1)\times(1-p^{-1})\,,

where
\zeta_{p}(s)
is the
p
-adic Riemann zeta function, which has a pole at
s=1
with residue
1-p^{-1}
.

6.4.5.

According to
[
Das16
, Theorem 9.4]
, the two variable
p
-adic
L
-function

L_{p}(\ad\,\underline{\sigma})(\lambda,s):=L_{p}(\underline{\sigma}\otimes\underline{\sigma}^{c})(\lambda,\lambda,\wt(\lambda)-1+s)

verifies a functional equation

(6.14)

L_{p}(\ad\,\underline{\sigma})(\lambda,s)=\epsilon(\ad\underline{\sigma}_{\lambda})(s)\cdot L_{p}(\ad\,\underline{\sigma}(1))(\lambda,-s)\,,

where
L_{p}(\ad\,\underline{\sigma}(1))(\lambda,s):=L_{p}(\ad\,\underline{\sigma})(\lambda,1+s)
, and
\epsilon(\ad\underline{\sigma}_{\lambda})(s)
is the epsilon factor described in
[
Das16
, Theorem 9.4]
. More precisely, for any crystalline specialisation
\lambda
of
\underline{\sigma}
, we have
\epsilon(\ad\underline{\sigma}_{\lambda})(s)=A_{\lambda}\cdot B_{\lambda}^{\wt(\lambda)-s}
, where
A_{\lambda}\in\overline{\mathbb{Q}}^{\times}
and
B_{\lambda}
is the conductor of the Gelbart–Jacquet lift of
\underline{\sigma}_{\lambda}
.

Let us put
\epsilon(\ad\underline{\sigma}_{\lambda}):=\epsilon(\ad\underline{\sigma}_{\lambda})(0)
. Substituting
s=0
in (
6.14
), we obtain

(6.15)

L_{p}(\ad\,\underline{\sigma})_{|s=0}(\lambda)=\epsilon(\ad\underline{\sigma}_{\lambda})\cdot L_{p}(\ad\,\underline{\sigma}(1))_{|s=0}(\lambda)=L_{p}(\ad\,\underline{\sigma})_{|s=1}(\lambda)\,.

6.4.6.

We assume until the end of our paper that any one of the following equivalent conditions holds true:

i)

The family
\underline{\sigma}
is non-CM.

ii)

\underline{\sigma}_{\lambda}
is non-CM for some (any) classical specialisation
\lambda
of
\sigma
of weight at least
2
.

iii)

If there exists a Dirichlet character
\chi=\chi_{\lambda}
such that for some (any) classical specialisation
\lambda
of
\sigma
of weight at least
2
we have
\underline{\sigma}_{\lambda}\simeq\underline{\sigma}_{\lambda}\otimes\chi
, then
\chi=\mathds{1}
is the trivial character.

iv)

\ad^{0}\rho_{\lambda}
is irreducible for for some (any) classical specialisation
\lambda
of
\sigma
of weight at least
2
.

The equivalence of (i) and (ii) follows from
[
GV04
, Proposition 8]
, whereas the equivalence of (ii) and (iii) is well-known; cf.
[
Rib77
,
Rib80
]
. Finally, the equivalence of (iii) and (iv) is an immediate consequence of Schur’s lemma.

In this case, the epsilon factor (at
s=0
)
\epsilon(\ad^{0}\underline{\sigma}_{\lambda})
interpolates to an analytic function
\epsilon(\ad^{0}\underline{\sigma})
of
\lambda
, cf.
[
Mun23
, Theorem C]
. As we have
\epsilon(\ad\underline{\sigma}_{\lambda})=\epsilon(\ad^{0}\underline{\sigma}_{\lambda})
(since the epsilon factor of the Riemann zeta function at
s=0
equals to
1
), it follows that
\epsilon(\ad\underline{\sigma}_{\lambda})
interpolates to an analytic function
\epsilon(\ad\underline{\sigma})=\epsilon(\ad^{0}\underline{\sigma})
of
\lambda
. We may then rewrite (
6.15
) as

(6.16)

L_{p}(\ad\,\underline{\sigma})_{|s=0}=\epsilon(\ad\underline{\sigma})\cdot L_{p}(\ad\,\underline{\sigma})_{|s=1}\,.

6.4.7.

Based on the observations in §
6.4.3
–§
6.4.5
, together with (
6.11
) and (
6.12
), we set

L_{p}^{(a)}(T_{?}^{\dagger})^{2}:=L_{p}^{(\underline{\sigma}^{c})}(T_{?,1}^{\dagger})^{2}\times L_{p}^{(\underline{\sigma}^{c})}(T_{?,2}^{\dagger})\times L_{p}^{(\underline{\sigma}^{c})}(T_{?,3}^{\dagger})\,,\qquad?=2,3\,.

6.4.8.

We can finally concluse with the following factorisation statement. Note that
L_{p}^{(\underline{\sigma}^{c})}(T_{3,2}^{\dagger})
is the
p
-adic
L
-function
L_{p}(\ad\,\underline{\sigma})_{|s=0}
(of Hida) and
L_{p}^{(\underline{\sigma}^{c})}(T_{3,3}^{\dagger})
equals
L_{p}(\ad\,\underline{\sigma}(1))_{|s=0}=L_{p}(\ad\,\underline{\sigma})_{|s=1}
.

Proposition 6.22
.

In the setting of the present subsection, assume that
[
BCPdVP25
, Conjecture 5.1]
(extension of Gross–Kudla conjecture) holds true. Then,

\displaystyle L_{p}^{(a)}(T_{3}^{\dagger})^{2}

\displaystyle:=L_{p}^{(\underline{\sigma}^{c})}(T_{3,1}^{\dagger})^{2}\times L_{p}^{(\underline{\sigma}^{c})}(T_{3,2}^{\dagger})\times L_{p}^{(\underline{\sigma}^{c})}(T_{3,3}^{\dagger})

\displaystyle=L_{p}^{\rm bal}(M_{3,1}^{\dagger})\times L_{p}({\rm ad}\,\underline{\sigma})_{|s=0}\times L_{p}({\rm ad}\,\underline{\sigma}(1))_{|s=0}\times\Log(\Delta_{{\underline{\mathbf{f}}}}^{\dagger})^{2}\qquad\pmod{{\mathcal{R}}_{{\underline{\mathbf{f}}}}[1/p]^{\times}}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\left(\epsilon(\ad\underline{\sigma})\cdot L_{p}^{\rm bal}(M_{3,1}^{\dagger})\times L_{p}^{\prime}({\rm ad}^{0}\underline{\sigma})_{|s=1}^{2}\right)}_{\Log_{\omega_{M_{3}}}(\delta_{M_{3}^{\dagger}}^{(c)})^{2}}\times\underbrace{\left(\Log(\Delta_{{\underline{\mathbf{f}}}}^{\dagger})^{2}\times(1-p^{-1})^{2}\right)}_{\Log_{\omega_{\underline{\Pi}}}(\Delta_{\underline{\Pi}}^{\dagger})^{2}}\qquad\pmod{{\mathcal{R}}_{{\underline{\mathbf{f}}}}[1/p]^{\times}}\,.

As in the case of Theorem
6.21
, slightly more is true: The ambiguity (which is a factor in
{\mathcal{R}}_{{\underline{\mathbf{f}}}}[1/p]^{\times}
) specialises at classical points to an explicit algebraic number.

Proof.

The factorisation

L_{p}^{(\underline{\sigma}^{c})}(T_{3,1}^{\dagger})=\mathcal{C}\cdot L_{p}^{\rm bal}(M_{3,1}^{\dagger})\times\Log(\Delta_{\underline{\mathbf{f}}}^{\dagger})

is proved in
[
BS25
,
BCPdVP25
]
under the running hypotheses, whereas

(6.17)

L_{p}^{(\underline{\sigma}^{c})}(T_{3,3}^{\dagger})=L_{p}(\ad\,\underline{\sigma}(1))_{|s=0}=L_{p}^{\prime}({\rm ad}^{0}\underline{\sigma}(1))_{|_{s=0}}\times(1-p^{-1})=L_{p}^{\prime}({\rm ad}^{0}\underline{\sigma})_{|_{s=1}}\times(1-p^{-1})

is a consequence of Dasgupta’s results
[
Das16
]
that we have reviewed in §
6.4.4
and §
6.4.5
. Finally,

L_{p}^{(\underline{\sigma}^{c})}(T_{3,2}^{\dagger})=L_{p}(\ad\,\underline{\sigma})_{|s=0}=\epsilon(\ad\underline{\sigma})\cdot L_{p}(\ad\,\underline{\sigma})_{|s=1}=\epsilon(\ad\underline{\sigma})\cdot L_{p}^{\prime}({\rm ad}^{0}\underline{\sigma})_{|s=1}\times(1-p^{-1})\,,

where the second equality follows from (
6.16
), and the third from (
6.17
).
∎

6.4.9.

We compare the factorisation statement in Proposition
6.22
with that in Conjecture
2.10
in the generic scenario, explaining the meaning of
\Log_{\omega_{M_{3}}}(\delta_{M_{3}^{\dagger}}^{(c)})
and
\Log_{\omega_{\underline{\Pi}}}(\Delta_{\underline{\Pi}}^{\dagger})
.

In Proposition
6.22
, we have set
\Delta_{{\underline{\Pi}}}^{\dagger}:=(1-p^{-1})\,\Delta_{{\underline{\mathbf{f}}}}^{\dagger}
, where
\Delta_{{\underline{\mathbf{f}}}}^{\dagger}
is the family of Heegner cycles as before. The cohomology class
\delta_{M_{3}^{\dagger}}^{(c)}
is expected to arise as an “improved Beilinson–Flach class”
\BF_{\ad\,\underline{\sigma}(1)}^{*}
scaled by the square-root of
\epsilon(\ad\underline{\sigma})\cdot L_{p}^{\rm bal}(M_{3,1}^{\dagger})
. We remark that the Beilinson–Flach elemement associated to
\ad^{0}T_{\underline{\sigma}}(1)
vanishes identically (due to the presence of an exceptional zero), and one expects an explicit reciprocity law linking
\BF_{\ad\,\underline{\sigma}(1)}^{*}
to

L_{p}(\ad\,\underline{\sigma})_{|s=0}=\epsilon(\ad\underline{\sigma})\cdot(1-p^{-1})\cdot L_{p}^{\prime}(\ad^{0}\underline{\sigma})_{|s=1}\,,

where the equality above follows from (
6.13
) combined with (
6.16
). The construction of the improved class
\BF_{\ad\,\underline{\sigma}(1)}^{*}
as well as the reciprocity law alluded to above will be addressed in future work.

6.4.10.

Observe that in this case, the algebraic counterpart of Proposition
6.22
reduces to a combination of Palvannan’s main results and
[
Pal18
]
and
[
BS25
, Theorem 6.7]
.

6.4.11.

The factorisation problem for
L_{p}(16d)
in the setting of §
6.4
reduces to the tautological equality
0=0
as a consequence of our assumptions on
\varepsilon
-factors.
